91 [SOURCE: Page_91]

Abstract: Consequences of plant-insect interactions may vary depending on [SOURCE: Page_92]

nutritional status of the plants as well as the plant cultivars. We hypothesize that (i) [SOURCE: Page_92]

drought adaptation and nitrogen-deficiency regulation capacity in tomato cultivars [SOURCE: Page_92]

may mitigate the bottom-up effects of drought and nitrogen deficiency on herbivorous [SOURCE: Page_92]

species, and (ii) the herbivorous insects from different feeding guild may respond [SOURCE: Page_92]

differently depending on the stress type (i.e. drought or nitrogen deficiency) and plant [SOURCE: Page_92]

cultivar. Here, we evaluated the performance of two herbivorous insects from distinct [SOURCE: Page_92]

feeding guilds, the leaf miner Tuta absoluta and the phloem feeder Bemisia tabaci, [SOURCE: Page_92]

feeding on the two tomato cultivars (cv. Marmande (M) vs. cv. Noir de Crimée (NC)) [SOURCE: Page_92]

under optimal/insufficient nitrogen and optimal/insufficient water inputs (i.e., [SOURCE: Page_92]

drought). We found that (i) cv. NC appears less sensitive to drought stress (i.e more [SOURCE: Page_92]

drought tolerant) than cv. M in terms of several leaf gas-exchanges traits as well as [SOURCE: Page_92]

glycoalkaloids, whereas cv. NC is more sensitive to nitrogen deficiency than cv. M in [SOURCE: Page_92]

terms of leaf nitrogen status and glycoalkaloids; (ii) Drought adaptation in cv. NC did [SOURCE: Page_92]

not help mitigate the negative bottom-up effects of drought on T. absoluta. Similarly, a [SOURCE: Page_92]

stronger capacity of regulating nitrogen deficiency in cv. M did not help mitigate the [SOURCE: Page_92]

bottom-up effects of nitrogen limitation on T. absoluta and B. tabaci. (iii) Irrespective [SOURCE: Page_92]

of plant cultivar, both nitrogen and water deficit significantly affected T. absoluta [SOURCE: Page_92]

survival and development. However, B. tabaci survival was only negatively affected [SOURCE: Page_92]

by the nitrogen deficiency, not the water deficit. The patterns of these negative [SOURCE: Page_92]

responses of the insects were similar on both cultivars. Therefore, our results reject [SOURCE: Page_92]

our first hypothesis, but partially support the second. [SOURCE: Page_92]

Key words: Plant-insect interaction, Agrosystem, Irrigation, Nitrogen fertilization, [SOURCE: Page_92]

Plant defenses, Glycoalkaloid, Solanum lycopersicum, Insect feeding guilds 92 [SOURCE: Page_92]

Introduction [SOURCE: Page_93]

In terrestrial ecosystems, various biotic (i.e. environmental stress tolerance in plants [SOURCE: Page_93]

and insect resistance in plants etc.) or various abiotic factors (e.g. nutrients, resource [SOURCE: Page_93]

quality and quantity etc.) may act as crucial bottom-up forces in influencing natural [SOURCE: Page_93]

communities (Hunter and Price 1992). Among these factors, plant nutritional [SOURCE: Page_93]

conditions (i.e. resource quality and quantity) act as one of the most important [SOURCE: Page_93]

bottom-up forces influencing plant-insect interactions (Hunter and Price 1992; Denno [SOURCE: Page_93]

et al. 2002; Costamagna and Landis 2006), such as nitrogen and water inputs (Inbar et [SOURCE: Page_93]

al. 2001). For instance, when plants were grown under drought or non-optimal [SOURCE: Page_93]

nitrogen input conditions, plant nutritional quality as well as defensive compounds [SOURCE: Page_93]

(both quantity and quality) may vary (Inbar et al. 2001; Gutbrodt et al. 2011; Royer et [SOURCE: Page_93]

al. 2013), and consequently trigger bottom-up effects on herbivorous insects that feed [SOURCE: Page_93]

on them. However, these bottom-up effects of resources input/availability to plants are [SOURCE: Page_93]

highly variable and may depend on various factors: type of resource, intensity of [SOURCE: Page_93]

stress, plant cultivar, feeding strategy of feeding species as well as feeding [SOURCE: Page_93]

specialization (Inbar et al. 2001; Huberty and Denno 2004; Mody et al, 2009; [SOURCE: Page_93]

Gutbrodt et al. 2011). [SOURCE: Page_93]

Water is one of the crucial resources for both plants and insects that feed on them. [SOURCE: Page_93]

Varying water availability to plants may substantially induced physiological changes [SOURCE: Page_93]

in terms of nutritional values and chemical defense in plants, thus affecting herbivores [SOURCE: Page_93]

insects (Koricheva et al. 1998; Gutbrodt et al. 2011; Tariq et al. 2012). Effects of [SOURCE: Page_93]

drought on plant-herbivorous insect interactions is primarily dependent on insect [SOURCE: Page_93]

feeding strategy, insect feeding specialization as well as modalities of drought (Inbar [SOURCE: Page_93]

et al. 2001; Huberty and Denno 2004; Mody et al, 2009; Gutbrodt et al. 2011). Firstly, 93 [SOURCE: Page_93]

different insect feeding strategies (i.e. phloem-feeding, chewing, or leaf-mining etc.) [SOURCE: Page_94]

may influence the insect responses to drought. Generally, leafminer and leaf chewers [SOURCE: Page_94]

are considered to be more sensitive to drought than phloem feeders (Larsson 1989; [SOURCE: Page_94]

Inbar et al. 2001). Thus two primary hypotheses Plant vigor hypothesis (Price 1991) [SOURCE: Page_94]

and the Plant stress hypothesis (White 1993) were developed to explain such [SOURCE: Page_94]

discrepancy. The former predicts that herbivorous insects perform better on [SOURCE: Page_94]

rapidly-growing plants i.e. vigorous ones (Price 1991), and this hypothesis has been [SOURCE: Page_94]

supported particularly in leafminer and leaf chewer (Inbar et al. 2001; Han et al. 2014). [SOURCE: Page_94]

The latter predicts that water-stressed plants are more suitable hosts for [SOURCE: Page_94]

senescence-feeders that feed preferentially on overmature and senescing tissues [SOURCE: Page_94]

(White 2009). Secondly, the modalities of drought event are also important in [SOURCE: Page_94]

determining the consequences of drought on herbivorous insects. Pulsed Plant Stress [SOURCE: Page_94]

Hypothesis (PPSH) has been described to explain discrepancies between phloem [SOURCE: Page_94]

feeder outbreaks observed in situ on water-stressed plants and the negative effects on [SOURCE: Page_94]

phloem feeder detected in laboratory studies (Huberty and Denno 2004). PPSH [SOURCE: Page_94]

predicts that phloem feeder populations (e.g. aphids or whiteflies) respond positively [SOURCE: Page_94]

to plants that are under discontinuous stress where bouts of stress and recovery of [SOURCE: Page_94]

turgor allow phloem feeders to benefit from stress-induced increases in plant nitrogen, [SOURCE: Page_94]

however, phloem feeders may be negatively affected by continuous water stress. [SOURCE: Page_94]

Thirdly, the outcomes of drought on herbivorous insects also depend on insect feeding [SOURCE: Page_94]

specialization even if they adopt the same feeding strategy (Gutbrodt et al. 2011). [SOURCE: Page_94]

Drought event may increase susceptibility of plants particularly to generalist [SOURCE: Page_94]

herbivorous insects. [SOURCE: Page_94]

Nitrogen availability for plants is also a key factor influencing herbivorous insect [SOURCE: Page_94]

performance (Mattson 1980). Generally no consensus has been reached on the 94 [SOURCE: Page_94]

bottom-up effects of nitrogen inputs on herbivorous insects. The Nitrogen limitation [SOURCE: Page_95]

hypothesis (White 1993) predicts that reduced availability of nitrogen to plants may [SOURCE: Page_95]

impair the performance of Lepidoptera insects and this hypothesis has been supported [SOURCE: Page_95]

by numerous studies (White 1993; Hunter and McNeil 1997; Grundel et al 1998; [SOURCE: Page_95]

Inbar et al 2001; Cornelissen et al 2009; Han et al. 2014). However, several other [SOURCE: Page_95]

studies reported contradicting results that high nitrogen content level in leaves [SOURCE: Page_95]

compromised the biological traits in herbivores such as pupal survival, adult survival [SOURCE: Page_95]

and adult body size (Joern and Behmer 1998; Fischer and Fiedler 2000). For phloem [SOURCE: Page_95]

feeders, increase in nitrogen input and optimal plant nitrogen status generally [SOURCE: Page_95]

enhanced the performance of phloem-feeding insects, such as whiteflies, thrips etc. [SOURCE: Page_95]

(Jauset et al. 1998; Bentz et al. 1995 a, b; Bi et al 2001; 2003; Inbar et al 2001; Chau [SOURCE: Page_95]

et al. 2005; Hogendorp et al. 2006). [SOURCE: Page_95]

However, in modern agriculture, diverse plant cultivars have been selected to [SOURCE: Page_95]

cope with sub-optimal growing conditions (hereafter referred as “resistant cultivar”). [SOURCE: Page_95]

Plant adaptation to various abiotic stresses (i.e. drought and nitrogen deficiency) [SOURCE: Page_95]

involve complex physiological mechanisms (Cattivelli et al. 2008; Farooq et al. 2009; [SOURCE: Page_95]

Feng et al. 2009). For instance, enormous effects have been directed to improve the [SOURCE: Page_95]

drought-adaptation in plants by increasing the water-use efficiency (Galmés et al. [SOURCE: Page_95]

2013). Acclimation to drought conditions may comprise long-term adjustments [SOURCE: Page_95]

involving gene expression and modification of plant physiology, biochemistry, [SOURCE: Page_95]

morphology and anatomy (Chaves et al. 2002; Flexas et al. 2006; Chaves et al. 2009). [SOURCE: Page_95]

However, to date, it is unclear how all these changes occurred in resistant cultivars [SOURCE: Page_95]

may influence the potential bottom-up effects of drought on herbivorous insects; in [SOURCE: Page_95]

contrast, drought generally compromises herbivorous insects that feed on [SOURCE: Page_95]

drought-susceptible cultivars (Inbar et al. 2001; Han et al. 2014). Therefore, it may be 95 [SOURCE: Page_95]

a valuable attempt to consider plant cultivars with drought-resistance or [SOURCE: Page_96]

nitrogen-deficiency tolerance traits in better understanding the bottom-up effects of [SOURCE: Page_96]

water and nitrogen inputs on plant-herbivorous insect interactions. [SOURCE: Page_96]

In order to understand the bottom-up effects of variable environments on [SOURCE: Page_96]

plant-insect interaction, a “nitrogen / water-tomato plant-herbivorous insects” testing [SOURCE: Page_96]

system was set up. The cultivar 'Marmande' (referred as cv. M in the following) is a [SOURCE: Page_96]

commonly-used cultivar in local farming and organic farming under greenhouse in [SOURCE: Page_96]

France and is also our plant model, often used in our previous studies on plant-insect [SOURCE: Page_96]

interactions (Chailleux 2013a, 2013b; Jaworski et al. 2013; Han et al 2014). The [SOURCE: Page_96]

cultivar “Noire de Crimée” (hereafter referred as cv. NC) is also a commonly used [SOURCE: Page_96]

cultivar in France and is sold as "drought-tolerant cultivar" by numerous seed sellers. [SOURCE: Page_96]

Thus in the present study, three main questions were proposed: (i) Do cv. M and [SOURCE: Page_96]

cv. NC respond differently to drought and nitrogen deficiency in terms of chemistry, [SOURCE: Page_96]

physiological traits as well as chemical defense against herbivores (i.e. with one [SOURCE: Page_96]

cultivar more tolerant to drought/nitrogen deficiency than the other)? (ii) Since the [SOURCE: Page_96]

negative bottom-up effects of drought and nitrogen deficiency on a Lepidoptera Tuta [SOURCE: Page_96]

absoluta    (Meytick)    (Lepidoptera:     Gelechiidae)    has    been    identified   on [SOURCE: Page_96]

drought-susceptible tomato cultivar (cv. M) (Han et al. 2014), do drought-tolerance [SOURCE: Page_96]

and nitrogen-regulation traits in either cv. M or cv. NC mitigate such bottom-up [SOURCE: Page_96]

effects on this Lepidoptera? (ii) If the bottom-up effects do not mitigate on these [SOURCE: Page_96]

resistant cultivars, do the insect species from distinct feeding guild (i.e. leafminer and [SOURCE: Page_96]

phloem feeder) respond differently to the related stresses? We hypothesize that (i) [SOURCE: Page_96]

drought tolerance and nitrogen regulation traits in tomato cultivars may mitigate the [SOURCE: Page_96]

bottom-up effects of drought and nitrogen deficiency on herbivorous species, and (ii) [SOURCE: Page_96]

the herbivorous insects from different feeding guild may respond differently 96 [SOURCE: Page_96]

depending on the stress type (i.e. drought or nitrogen deficiency) and plant cultivar. [SOURCE: Page_97]

Material and Methods [SOURCE: Page_97]

Biological material [SOURCE: Page_97]

Both cultivars ('Marmande', cv. M and 'Noire de Crimée' cv. NC) of Tomato (Solanum [SOURCE: Page_97]

lycopersicum L.) were grown from seeds in a climatic chamber (12 h light, 241°C, [SOURCE: Page_97]

655% RH) in cubic plastic pots (776.5 cm). The plants were then grown under [SOURCE: Page_97]

laboratory conditions (16 h light, 241°C, 655% RH). At DAS 8 ( Days After [SOURCE: Page_97]

Seeding) the seedlings were washed to remove soil and then transferred to new cubic [SOURCE: Page_97]

pots containing limestone grains (Perlite Italiana srl, Corsico, Italy) (see schedule Fig. [SOURCE: Page_97]

1). At DAS 24, the plants were transferred to larger pots (diameter: 10 cm, height: 9 [SOURCE: Page_97]

cm) filled with the same substrate. [SOURCE: Page_97]

The T. absoluta colony was maintained in regular chambers using tomato plants [SOURCE: Page_97]

(25 ± 1°C, RH 70 ± 10%, 16 h light) (Han et al. 2014). Honey and water were [SOURCE: Page_97]

provided ad libitum to adults in rearing cages. Newly-oviposited T. absoluta eggs (≤ [SOURCE: Page_97]

24 h) were used to infest the plants at DAS 40 (see Fig. 1). To obtain these eggs, we [SOURCE: Page_97]

used the method described by Chailleux et al (2013). Ten couples of T absoluta adults [SOURCE: Page_97]

were placed inside a plastic tube containing a fresh tomato leaf to enable mating and [SOURCE: Page_97]

oviposition. [SOURCE: Page_97]

The B. tabaci colony was maintained in regular chambers using young tobacco [SOURCE: Page_97]

plants (25 ± 1°C, RH 70 ± 10%, 16 h light). Honey and water were provided ad [SOURCE: Page_97]

libitum to adults in rearing cages. Two tobacco plants were put inside the cage at DAS [SOURCE: Page_97]

97 [SOURCE: Page_97]

35 to obtain 1st stage nymphs (crawlers) for tomato plants infestation at DAS 45 (see [SOURCE: Page_98]

Fig. 1). [SOURCE: Page_98]

Nitrogen and water input [SOURCE: Page_98]

We used the same nitrogen and water inputs regime that used in our previous study [SOURCE: Page_98]

(Han et al. 2014), but only two levels of nitrogen inputs (ON: optimal nitrogen; IN: [SOURCE: Page_98]

insufficient nitrogen) and two levels of water inputs (OW: optimal water; DT: drought, [SOURCE: Page_98]

i.e. the “HD”-“high drought” in Han et al. 2014). The treatments started at DAS 8 and [SOURCE: Page_98]

ended at DAS 60 (Figure S$_2$). [SOURCE: Page_98]

The amounts of nutrient inputs and volumes of feeding solution supplied to plants [SOURCE: Page_98]

increased daily in an exponential way depending on the plant development stage [SOURCE: Page_98]

(Ingestad and Agren, 1995; Le Bot et al. 1998). Three stock solutions to provide [SOURCE: Page_98]

nitrate, phosphate and sulphate salts independently (+ Fe & micronutrients) were [SOURCE: Page_98]

mixed and applied to the plants (see the detailed formula in Han et al. 2014). To [SOURCE: Page_98]

differentiate N inputs, two different doses, vn and vn/5 of the nitrate stock solution [SOURCE: Page_98]

were used daily to fertilize the plants. These doses were added to the water intake (see [SOURCE: Page_98]

below) of each pot in order to set the optimal nitrogen (ON) and insufficient nitrogen [SOURCE: Page_98]

(IN) inputs respectively (Figure S$_2$ A). [SOURCE: Page_98]

A “step increase” pattern was used for daily water inputs (Figure S$_2$ B). Daily [SOURCE: Page_98]

optimal water (OW) input was determined empirically as the amount required to [SOURCE: Page_98]

fully-saturate the perlite substrate without visible drainage. Based on vw, [SOURCE: Page_98]

complementary volume vw/3 was applied daily to set the drought (DT) treatment per [SOURCE: Page_98]

plant. All nutrient solutions were adjusted to pH 5.5 by using H2SO$_4$ (in 0.2 M). In our [SOURCE: Page_98]

study, DT was considered as “pulsed water stress” because the plants received [SOURCE: Page_98]

98 [SOURCE: Page_98]

solution daily i.e. sporadically and a tutor recovery was expected in plant leaves since [SOURCE: Page_99]

no evident symptom of wilting was observed (Huberty and Denno 2004). [SOURCE: Page_99]

Experimental setup [SOURCE: Page_99]

Figure S$_1$ shows the schedule of all the events recorded during the experiment. This [SOURCE: Page_99]

schedule repeated for two times (2 trials) except that the leaf gas-exchange [SOURCE: Page_99]

measurement (LGE) was only performed in the second trial. [SOURCE: Page_99]

Leaf gas-exchange measurement - CO$_2$ assimilation, transpiration and stomatal [SOURCE: Page_99]

conductance measurements were performed on three well-developed terminal leaflets [SOURCE: Page_99]

(one leaflet per plant) per treatment at DAS 38. They were measured with a portable [SOURCE: Page_99]

photosynthesis systems (Li-6400, Li-Cor, Lincoln, NE, USA) equipped with a light [SOURCE: Page_99]

source (6200-02B LED, Li-Cor). Leaves were first acclimated in the chamber for [SOURCE: Page_99]

more than 20 min under controlled conditions: Leaf temperature of 27°C, air CO$_2$ [SOURCE: Page_99]

concentration of 400 µmol CO$_2$ mol-1 air and saturating photosynthetic photon flux [SOURCE: Page_99]

density (PPFD) of 700 µmol h m–2 s–1. [SOURCE: Page_99]

Leaf sampling - At DAS 43, the 4th fully-developed leaf from the top from each [SOURCE: Page_99]

plant were sampled. These leaves were immediately dried at 60°C for 72 h for the [SOURCE: Page_99]

quantification of leaf N and glycoalkaloids content. [SOURCE: Page_99]

Leaf N content and C/N ratio quantification - To analyse the nitrogen and carbon [SOURCE: Page_99]

contents (dry mass percent), the dried leaf samples were ground into fine powder [SOURCE: Page_99]

using a ball mill MM301 (Retsch, Germany) and stored individually in small tubes. [SOURCE: Page_99]

Leaf nitrogen and carbon contents were measured with an elemental analyser (Flash [SOURCE: Page_99]

EA1112 Series, ThermoFinnigan, Milan, Italy) at INRA-PSH Avignon. The leaf C/N [SOURCE: Page_99]

ratio was calculated as the ratio between leaf carbon content (%) and leaf nitrogen [SOURCE: Page_99]

content (%). 99 [SOURCE: Page_99]

Leaf glycoalkaloid quantification - We used the same powder samples from dried [SOURCE: Page_100]

leaves for glycoalkaloid analysis as those used for leaf N content quantification. [SOURCE: Page_100]

Glycoalkaloids were extracted from dried tomato leaves (5 g) mixed with 2 mL of 5% [SOURCE: Page_100]

acetic acid (CH3COOH) in water (v/v). The suspension was first mixed with a vortex [SOURCE: Page_100]

and then extracted twice for 30 min using an ultrasonic assisted extractor (Reus) at [SOURCE: Page_100]

room temperature. After the extraction, the supernatant was filtered through a 0.45 µm [SOURCE: Page_100]

PVDF PuradiscTM filters (Whatman, GE Healthcare). All samples were kept at -20°C [SOURCE: Page_100]

until the LC analyses. Glycoalkaloid standards (α-tomatine and tomatidine) were also [SOURCE: Page_100]

diluted in 5% CH3COOH solution. [SOURCE: Page_100]

All analyses were performed on an Ultimate 3000 Rapid Separation LC (RSLC) [SOURCE: Page_100]

systems (Thermo Scientific) equipped with a PDA detector and hyphenated to an [SOURCE: Page_100]

ESI-Q-TOF mass spectrometer (microTOFQII, Bruker Daltonique). Separation was [SOURCE: Page_100]

carried out on an Ascentis Express Fused-Core™ C18 column (100 × 2.1mm i.d., 2.7 [SOURCE: Page_100]

μm; Supelco) with its corresponding guard column (Ascentis express, 2.1 mm id x 50 [SOURCE: Page_100]

mm, 2.7 µm, Supelco). After optimisation, a gradient elution program was developed [SOURCE: Page_100]

enabling separation of glycoalkaloids. The flow rate was set at 400µL/min and the [SOURCE: Page_100]

solvent system was (A) water (H2O) 0.1% acid formic (AF, 0.1% v/v) and (B) [SOURCE: Page_100]

acetonitrile (ACN) 0.1% AF (v/v). The elution program was: 2% B for 5 min, 50% B [SOURCE: Page_100]

for 35 min, 100% B for 5 min and hold for 3 min, back to 2% B in 5 min and [SOURCE: Page_100]

conditioning for 2.5 min. The column oven was thermostated at 35°C and the [SOURCE: Page_100]

autosampler at 6°C. The injection volume was set at 5 µL. [SOURCE: Page_100]

Before analyses, the mass spectrometer was calibrated in an external mode using [SOURCE: Page_100]

a mix of known mass (ESI-L Low concentration Tuning Mix, Agilent Technologies). [SOURCE: Page_100]

HRMS data were acquired in positive ionisation and in MS scan modes. The source [SOURCE: Page_100]

temperature was set at 195°C, the capillary voltage at 3.8kV, nebuliser gaz (nitrogen) 100 [SOURCE: Page_100]

at 2.8 bar and dry gaz (nitrogen) at 9 L/min. Mass spectra acquisition was set at 5000 [SOURCE: Page_101]

spectra/sec on a mass range of 50-2000 m/z. LC-MS raw data are processed using [SOURCE: Page_101]

Data Analysis 4.1 software (ESI Compass 1.5, Bruker Daltonique). An ion extraction [SOURCE: Page_101]

method using a mass window of 0.01 Da was used for the quantification of [SOURCE: Page_101]

glycoalkaloids. To obtain the corresponding quantity, the measured ion abundance [SOURCE: Page_101]

was reported on a standard curve (for α -tomatine and tomatidine) obtained in the [SOURCE: Page_101]

same condition of analysis and reprocessing. [SOURCE: Page_101]

Plant infestation - At DAS 40 (see Fig. 1), one leaf, the 3rd fully-developed leaf [SOURCE: Page_101]

(with 5 leaflets) from the top, from each plant was selected to be infested with one [SOURCE: Page_101]

newly-oviposited T. absoluta egg (≤ 24 h). The eggs were checked daily until larvae [SOURCE: Page_101]

hatched. If an egg failed to hatch, a newly-hatched larva (< 6h old) was released on [SOURCE: Page_101]

the leaf. To avoid larvae escaping, each infested leaf was then bagged with a nylon [SOURCE: Page_101]

mesh (0.2 mm, 30  24 cm). The T. absoluta infestation was performed on 96 plants. [SOURCE: Page_101]

At DAS 45 (see Fig. 1), the other group of 96 plants were infested by B. tabaci [SOURCE: Page_101]

crawlers. Six crawlers were released on the 3rd fully-developed leaf for each plant. [SOURCE: Page_101]

They were checked until all of them started walking and piercing leaf tissues. [SOURCE: Page_101]

Biological traits of T. absoluta and B. tabaci - For T. absoluta, survival and [SOURCE: Page_101]

development time of each life stage were recorded. Pupa mass were recorded by [SOURCE: Page_101]

weighing the pupae. For B. tabaci, mortality of crawlers and numbers reaching 2nd [SOURCE: Page_101]

nymph stage were recorded after 7 days of infestation. [SOURCE: Page_101]

Data analyses [SOURCE: Page_101]

Multivariate analysis of variance (MANOVA) test were performed to analyze the [SOURCE: Page_101]

effects of trial, nitrogen, water and cultivar on glycoalkaloids quantifications in leaf [SOURCE: Page_101]

dry mass. Factorial ANOVA (“nitrogen”, “water” and “cultivar”) were performed to 101 [SOURCE: Page_101]

test the effects of nitrogen, water and cultivar on plant traits including leaf N content, [SOURCE: Page_102]

leaf C/N ratio, glycoalkaloid compounds and insect traits including T. absoluta pupal [SOURCE: Page_102]

weight, development time from egg to pupa and from egg to adult, B. tabaci mortality [SOURCE: Page_102]

and survival rate reaching 2nd nymph stage. Shapiro and Bartlett tests were used to [SOURCE: Page_102]

assess variance homogeneity and normality, respectively. Independent samples t-test [SOURCE: Page_102]

was used to examine the difference in response of CO$_2$ assimilation, leaf transpiration [SOURCE: Page_102]

rate and stomatal conductance to drought among the two cultivars. Only the data [SOURCE: Page_102]

collected from “ON” (optimal nitrogen) were used in impact of order to focus on the [SOURCE: Page_102]

impact of drought. Independent samples t-test was used to examine the response of [SOURCE: Page_102]

leaf N content and C/N ratio between optimal nitrogen (ON) and insufficient nitrogen [SOURCE: Page_102]

(IN) (i.e. ON vs. IN) for both water treatments. Tukey post hoc test for multiple [SOURCE: Page_102]

comparisons were performed to examine the effect of nitrogen, water and cultivar on [SOURCE: Page_102]

all the glycoalkaloid compounds, as well as development of T. absoluta and survival [SOURCE: Page_102]

of B. tabaci. Survival of T. absoluta was fitted to a log-linear model using the [SOURCE: Page_102]

permuted Fisher exact test (using “water”, “nitrogen” and “stage” as factors). All [SOURCE: Page_102]

these data were analysed using the R software (R Core Team 2009). [SOURCE: Page_102]

Results [SOURCE: Page_102]

Impact of variable water input on leaf-gas exchange parameters [SOURCE: Page_102]

Figure 1 showed the effect of drought on photosynthesis rate (CO$_2$ assimilation), [SOURCE: Page_102]

transpiration rate and stomatal conductance at leaf level for both cultivars. Cv. M [SOURCE: Page_102]

showed decreased photosynthesis rate, transpiration rate and stomatal conductance [SOURCE: Page_102]

under drought compared those measured under optimal water input (photosynthesis: 102 [SOURCE: Page_102]

P = 0.007, df = 20, t = 1.649; transpiration: P = 0.001, df = 20, t = 4.113; stomatal [SOURCE: Page_103]

conductance: P = 0.008, df = 20, t = 2.934). In contrast, cv. NC responded similarly [SOURCE: Page_103]

among optimal water input and drought in terms of transpiration rate and stomatal [SOURCE: Page_103]

conductance (transpiration: P = 0.394, df = 18, t = 0.873; stomatal conductance: P = [SOURCE: Page_103]

0.278, df = 18, t = 1.119). But, the photosynthesis rate significantly decreased under [SOURCE: Page_103]

drought compared to optimal water input (P = 0.003, df = 18, t = 3.494). [SOURCE: Page_103]

Impact of nitrogen, water and cultivars input on plant traits [SOURCE: Page_103]

Table 2 shows the MANOVA analysis of effects of various factors (i.e. trial, [SOURCE: Page_103]

nitrogen, water, cultivar) on the quantifications of glycoalkoloids. A significant effect [SOURCE: Page_103]

of “trial” was found. However, we did not find strong interaction between the factor [SOURCE: Page_103]

“trial” with the factor “nitrogen’, “water” or “cultivar” (Figure S$_3$, S$_4$, S$_5$). This [SOURCE: Page_103]

indicated that a similar effect pattern of these factors on glycoalkaloids between the [SOURCE: Page_103]

two trials. Thus, we polled the data of glycoalkaloids from the two trials for further [SOURCE: Page_103]

analysis. Also, data of leaf N content and C/N ratio did not differed between the two [SOURCE: Page_103]

trials (F$_1$, 176 = 0.298; P = 0.586), thus the data from two trials were pooled. Factorial [SOURCE: Page_103]

ANOVA on various plant traits were summarized in Table 1. [SOURCE: Page_103]

Leaf N content and C/N ratio –Both leaf N content and C/N ratio were affected [SOURCE: Page_103]

by nitrogen inputs, but not the water inputs. A strong interaction between nitrogen and [SOURCE: Page_103]

cultivar on these two parameters was found (Table 1). Leaf N content in cv. NC was [SOURCE: Page_103]

significantly lower than that in cv. M (Fig. 3; IN-OW: P = 0.001, df = 46, t = 3.411; [SOURCE: Page_103]

IN-DT: P < 0.0001, df = 46, t = 5.102). Leaf C/N ratio in cv. NC was significantly [SOURCE: Page_103]

higher than that in cv. M (Fig. 3; P < 0.0001, df = 46, t = -5.207; IN-DT: P < 0.0001, [SOURCE: Page_103]

df = 46, t = -3.928). [SOURCE: Page_103]

Glycoalkaloid – Table S shows the concentrations of tomatidine, tomatine 1 and 103 [SOURCE: Page_103]

tomatine 2 quantified from the leaf dry mass on both tomato cultivars (except for [SOURCE: Page_104]

“Ddhydrotomatine”; no absolute value was available because of lack of standard [SOURCE: Page_104]

samples). Depending on the factorial ANOVA results (Table 1), we only present the [SOURCE: Page_104]

compounds that were significantly affected by the interaction effect of “nitrogen  [SOURCE: Page_104]

water”, “nitrogen  cultivar” and “water  cultivar”. Irrespective of cultivar, [SOURCE: Page_104]

concentration of tomatidine differed significantly among the treatments (ON-OW < [SOURCE: Page_104]

ON-DT = IN-OW < IN-DT) (Fig. 3). However, concentration of dehydrotomatine was [SOURCE: Page_104]

significantly lower only in ON-DT than other treatments (Fig. 3). Regarding the [SOURCE: Page_104]

“nitrogen  cultivar” effect on the four alkaloid compounds, the two cultivars [SOURCE: Page_104]

responded differently to the nitrogen treatment. Insufficient nitrogen input induced [SOURCE: Page_104]

higher concentrations of tomatidine, dehydrotomatine and tomatine 2 in cv. NC (all P [SOURCE: Page_104]

< 0.0001), but not in cv. M. Also, these three compounds were found higher in cv. M [SOURCE: Page_104]

than those in cv. NC (Fig. 4). Similarly, when examining “water  cultivar” effect on [SOURCE: Page_104]

alkaloids concentrations, the two cultivars responded differently to water treatments. [SOURCE: Page_104]

Drought induced higher concentrations of tomatidine and tomatine 1 in [SOURCE: Page_104]

cv. M, but not in cv. NC (Fig. 5). [SOURCE: Page_104]

Impact of nitrogen, water and cultivars input on insect biological traits [SOURCE: Page_104]

Factorial ANOVA on various insect biological traits were summarized in Table 3. [SOURCE: Page_104]

T. absoluta survival and development - Both nitrogen and water inputs [SOURCE: Page_104]

significantly affected T. absoluta survival from egg to pupa or to adult (nitrogen: χ2 = [SOURCE: Page_104]

9.55, df = 1, P = 0.002; water: χ2 = 7.64, df = 1, P = 0.006; nitrogen  water: χ2 = 0.00, [SOURCE: Page_104]

df = 1, P = 0.986) (Table 4). Tuta absoluta also showed different responses between [SOURCE: Page_104]

pupa and adult stages (stage: χ2 = 9.18, df = 1, P = 0.002). However, similar pattern [SOURCE: Page_104]

104 [SOURCE: Page_104]

was observed on both cultivars regarding the response of Tuta absoluta survival to [SOURCE: Page_105]

various treatments (cultivar: χ2 = 0.02, df = 1, P = 0.900). On both cultivars, T. [SOURCE: Page_105]

absoluta had a significantly lower survival rate in reaching any development stage [SOURCE: Page_105]

when the plants suffered insufficient nitrogen and drought treatment (IN-DT vs. [SOURCE: Page_105]

ON-OW, Table 4). On cv. M, drought to the plants resulted in a lower survival rate of [SOURCE: Page_105]

individuals reaching pupa even when the plants received optimal nitrogen (ON-DT vs. [SOURCE: Page_105]

ON-OW, Table 4). On cv. NC, sufficient nitrogen input to the plants resulted in a [SOURCE: Page_105]

lower survival rate of individuals reaching pupa even when the plants received [SOURCE: Page_105]

optimal water input (IN-OW vs. ON-OW, Table 4). [SOURCE: Page_105]

Table 3 and Fig. 6 showed that both nitrogen and water inputs significantly [SOURCE: Page_105]

affected T. absoluta development time from egg to pupa, from egg to adult and for [SOURCE: Page_105]

pupa weight. All the development-related traits had the similar pattern on both [SOURCE: Page_105]

cultivars in response to various treatments (P = 0.755, P = 0.724, P = 0.693 [SOURCE: Page_105]

respectively). The development time from egg to pupa or to adult was significantly [SOURCE: Page_105]

extended when the plants suffered drought and/or received insufficient nitrogen input [SOURCE: Page_105]

(all P < 0.05) (other treatments vs. ON-OW, Fig. 6). [SOURCE: Page_105]

B. tabaci survival- Only nitrogen significantly affected the mortality and number [SOURCE: Page_105]

of individuals reaching 2nd nymph (Table 3 and Fig. 7). The two traits had the similar [SOURCE: Page_105]

pattern on both cultivars in response to various treatments (P = 0.768 and P = 0.690, [SOURCE: Page_105]

respectively). Regardless of water input, insufficient nitrogen input significantly [SOURCE: Page_105]

increased B. tabaci mortality and decreased number of individuals reaching 2nd stage [SOURCE: Page_105]

(all P < 0.05) (IN-OW vs. ON-OW and IN-DT vs. ON-OW, Fig. 7). [SOURCE: Page_105]

105 [SOURCE: Page_105]

Discussion [SOURCE: Page_106]

Our current results provide clear answers to the questions proposed earlier in the [SOURCE: Page_106]

introduction. Firstly, we found strong evidence of plant resistance traits against [SOURCE: Page_106]

drought and nitrogen deficiency. Cultivar M was more tolerant to nitrogen deficiency [SOURCE: Page_106]

than cv. NC, whereas cv. NC was more tolerant to drought than cv. M. Secondly, [SOURCE: Page_106]

drought-tolerance and nitrogen-regulation traits in a resistance tomato cultivar did not [SOURCE: Page_106]

help to mitigate the negative bottom-up effects of drought and nitrogen deficiency on [SOURCE: Page_106]

the herbivorous insect T. absoluta. These negative bottom-up effects were retained on [SOURCE: Page_106]

both tomato cultivars even though either of the cultivar indeed showed drought [SOURCE: Page_106]

tolerance or nitrogen deficiency regulation. Thirdly, the herbivorous insects from [SOURCE: Page_106]

different feeding guild responded differently to the two types of stresses (i.e. drought [SOURCE: Page_106]

and nitrogen-defeciency), with T. absoluta negatively affected by both factors, but B. [SOURCE: Page_106]

tabaci only affected by nitrogen deficiency. However, their responses appeared [SOURCE: Page_106]

similar on two cultivars. Therefore, the current findings did not support the first [SOURCE: Page_106]

hypothesis, but partially support the second. [SOURCE: Page_106]

Cultivar M and cv. NC respond differently to drought and nitrogen [SOURCE: Page_106]

deficiency in terms of chemistry, physiological traits as well as chemical defense [SOURCE: Page_106]

against herbivores [SOURCE: Page_106]

Our findings suggested that the two tomato cultivars cv. M and cv. NC indeed [SOURCE: Page_106]

respond differently to drought and nitrogen deficiency. Firstly, we found strong [SOURCE: Page_106]

evidence that cv. NC was more tolerant to drought than cv. M.. Cultivar Marmande [SOURCE: Page_106]

was sensitive to drought with significantly decreased photosynthesis, transpiration [SOURCE: Page_106]

rate and stomatal conductance observed, but only photosynthesis rate was affected 106 [SOURCE: Page_106]

under drought cv. NC (Fig. 1). It may indicate that cv. M need to adjust water flux [SOURCE: Page_107]

under drought conditions while cv. NC need not (Farooq et al. 2009). Moreover, [SOURCE: Page_107]

drought induced higher concentrations of defensive compounds: tomatidine and [SOURCE: Page_107]

tomatine 1 in cv. M (Fig. 5). This agree with the fact that moderate drought often [SOURCE: Page_107]

causes an increase in the content of defensive compounds (Gutbrodt et al. 2011). [SOURCE: Page_107]

However, such effect was not found on cv. NC where the contents of defensive [SOURCE: Page_107]

compounds remained at equally low levels irrespective of water input. Secondly, in [SOURCE: Page_107]

contrast, cv. NC appeared more sensitive to nitrogen deficiency than cv. M because [SOURCE: Page_107]

leaf N content and leaf C/N in cv. NC vary with the nitrogen deficiency when cv. M [SOURCE: Page_107]

did not (Fig. 2). Also, nitrogen deficiency caused an increase in contents of tomatidine, [SOURCE: Page_107]

dehydrotomatine and tomatine 2 in cv. NC (Fig. 4). Such positive relationship [SOURCE: Page_107]

between glycoalkaloids content and leaf C/N ratio was consistent with the previous [SOURCE: Page_107]

report (Royer et al. 2013). However, since cv. M was less sensitive to nitrogen [SOURCE: Page_107]

deficiency, thus these defensive compounds did not differ among the nitrogen [SOURCE: Page_107]

treatments (Fig. 4). Overall, these results suggested that cv. M is somehow nitrogen [SOURCE: Page_107]

deficiency adaptive and cv. NC appears drought tolerance. [SOURCE: Page_107]

Bottom-up effects of drought and nitrogen deficiency retained on the [SOURCE: Page_107]

resistant cultivar [SOURCE: Page_107]

Negative bottom-up effects of drought on T. absoluta was found on cv. M in our [SOURCE: Page_107]

previous study (Han et al 2014), as well as the other Lepidoptera pests on other [SOURCE: Page_107]

drought-susceptible plants (White 1993; Inbar et al. 2001; Schoonhoven et al 2005; [SOURCE: Page_107]

Gutbrodt et al. 2011). These consequences have been attributed to the lower [SOURCE: Page_107]

nutritional value (from insect’s perspective) and higher defensive level. As the [SOURCE: Page_107]

glycoalkaloid was the main defense chemical in all organs (Royer et al. 2013), [SOURCE: Page_107]

increase in level of defensive compounds including tomatidine and tomatine 1 under 107 [SOURCE: Page_107]

drought observed in the current study may well explain such consequence (Fig. 3 and [SOURCE: Page_108]

Fig. 5). However, induction of higher levels of these defensive compounds was only [SOURCE: Page_108]

found in cv. M, not in cv. NC. In other words, lower chemical defense in cv. NC may [SOURCE: Page_108]

benefit the performance of T. absoluta. Despite this, it did not help cv. NC to mitigate [SOURCE: Page_108]

the negative bottom-up effects of drought on T. absoluta in comparison to cv. M [SOURCE: Page_108]

(Table 3, 4 and Fig. 6). [SOURCE: Page_108]

Likewise, nitrogen-regulation trait did not help cv. M to mitigate the negative [SOURCE: Page_108]

bottom-up effects of nitrogen deficiency on T. absoluta (Table 3, 4 and Fig. 6). [SOURCE: Page_108]

Specifically, the plant defensive level in term of glycoalkaloids was not increased by [SOURCE: Page_108]

nitrogen deficiency (Fig. 4). Thus, the negative effect of nitrogen deficiency could [SOURCE: Page_108]

only be linked to decreased nutritional value of leaves as food for T. absoluta. [SOURCE: Page_108]

Compared to optimal nitrogen input, the leaf N content in cv. M significantly [SOURCE: Page_108]

decreased due to nitrogen deficiency even though this value is still higher than that in [SOURCE: Page_108]

cv. NC. Hence, such sub-nutritious food may explain that the negative effects of [SOURCE: Page_108]

nitrogen deficiency on T. absoluta. [SOURCE: Page_108]

Insects from different feeding guilds responded differently to drought and [SOURCE: Page_108]

nitrogen deficiency [SOURCE: Page_108]

Herbivorous insects from different feeding guilds have different plant feeding [SOURCE: Page_108]

strategy and they may respond differentially to the source-based stressed plants (Inbar [SOURCE: Page_108]

et al. 2001; Huberty and Denno 2004). In the current study, both nitrogen and water [SOURCE: Page_108]

deficiency significantly affected T. absoluta survival and development on both tomato [SOURCE: Page_108]

cultivars. [SOURCE: Page_108]

However, the phloem feeder B. tabaci was only affected by the nitrogen [SOURCE: Page_108]

deficiency instead of water deficit. This agreed with the reports on negative bottom-up [SOURCE: Page_108]

effects of nitrogen-limited plants on whiteflies (Bentz et al. 1995 a, b; Inbar et al 2001; 108 [SOURCE: Page_108]

Bi et al 2001, 2003). We assumed that the whiteflies may face a lack of N-based [SOURCE: Page_109]

nutrient uptake on the nitrogen-limited plants because phloem feeders need to ingest a [SOURCE: Page_109]

diet rich in soluble carbohydrates as well as relatively high levels of free amino acids [SOURCE: Page_109]

from plants (Buchanan et al, 2000; Crafts-Brandner 2001). [SOURCE: Page_109]

However, water input variation did not affect the B. tabaci survival (see table 1 [SOURCE: Page_109]

and Fig. 6). Thus, our data did not support the “PPSH” hypothesis which predicted a [SOURCE: Page_109]

positive effects of pulsed water stressed plants on phloem feeders (Huberty and [SOURCE: Page_109]

Denno 2004). We hypothesize a trade-off theory to explain such neutral response. [SOURCE: Page_109]

Firstly, pulsed water stress induced an increase in phloem nitrogen predicted from the [SOURCE: Page_109]

PPSH theory (Huberty and Denno 2004; Mody et al, 2009). Thus, B. tabaci may [SOURCE: Page_109]

benefit from the increase in leaf phloem nitrogen content i.e. protein and free animo [SOURCE: Page_109]

acid. Secondly, however, the plants under drought conditions may contain less phloem [SOURCE: Page_109]

nutrients for B. tabaci because we found that drought treatment induced a [SOURCE: Page_109]

significantly lower foliar photosynthetic rate (Fig.1). Thus, these plants may [SOURCE: Page_109]

synthesize and accumulate less primary metabolites i.e. soluble carbohydrates in plant [SOURCE: Page_109]

phloem sap and the deficiency in primary metabolite-based nutrients may not be able [SOURCE: Page_109]

to meet the nutritional need of B. tabaci young nymphs. To conclude, these two [SOURCE: Page_109]

contrasting nutrient related forces may exert an offset effect of pulsed water stress on [SOURCE: Page_109]

quality of plant nutrients, which eventually resulted in a non-significant i.e. neutral [SOURCE: Page_109]

effect on B. tabaci. [SOURCE: Page_109]

The consistent negative effects of nitrogen deficiency on both insect species were [SOURCE: Page_109]

found. “Nitrogen limitation hypothesis” (White 1993) predicted that the herbivorous [SOURCE: Page_109]

insect performance was positively correlated with plant leaf N content. Our current [SOURCE: Page_109]

findings provide further evidence for this insect-plant interaction theory which has [SOURCE: Page_109]

been previously supported (Ravenscroft 1994; Obermaier and Zwölfer 1999; Inbar et 109 [SOURCE: Page_109]

al 2001; Han et al. 2014). Aside from the nitrogen-based nutrients, a suitable P: C [SOURCE: Page_110]

ratio (plants’ protein to carbohydrate ratio) of plant food is critical to growth and [SOURCE: Page_110]

development of many herbivorous insect (Simpson and Raubenheimer 1993; Bede et [SOURCE: Page_110]

al, 2007; Chen et al 2008). It was assumed that nitrogen limitation may reduce P;C [SOURCE: Page_110]

ratio in tomato leaves even though such ratio for T. absoluta and B. tabaci are still [SOURCE: Page_110]

unknown. Therefore, we conclude that negative effects of nitrogen limitation on the [SOURCE: Page_110]

herbivores may be attributed to the decrease in P:C ratio, or increase in concentrations [SOURCE: Page_110]

of plant chamecal defensive compounds such as tomatidine, dehydrotomatine and [SOURCE: Page_110]

tomatine 2 (Figure 4; Royer et al. 2013)as well as phenolics (Le Bot et al. 2009; [SOURCE: Page_110]

Larbat et al. 2012; Royer et al. 2013), or a combination of the two attributes. [SOURCE: Page_110]

137 [SOURCE: Page_137]

III) Bottom-up effects of nitrogen and water on predation performance of an [SOURCE: Page_138]

omnivorous predato [SOURCE: Page_138]

Our previous studies on plant-arthropod interactions have been limited within the first two trophic levels: plants and herbivorous insects. However, it is also important to include the organisms from level 3 (i.e., natural enemies) into our study, especially when we include the omnivorous predator into the model. Omnivorous feeding strategy i.e., omnivory plays an important role in community dynamics (McCann et al. 1998; Eubanks and Denno 2000; Sinia et al. 2004). Among these organisms, we intend to consider the species family Heteroptera because omnivory is common in most of Heteroptera predators (Naranjo and Gibson 1996; Coll 1998; Kaplan and Thaler 2011). Furthermore, many species from this family have been largely used as predators against a wide range of pests in biological control programs (Coll 1998; Sinia et al. 2004). At least, some of them are omnivores which not only target prey animals, but also damage plants (Janssen et al. 2003). The omnivory adopted by these predators makes the study modeling more complex. Heteroptera omnivorous predators acquire nutrients from prey animals, but alsowater by feeding on plants. Numerous studies have showed that lower plant nutritional quality increased the predation on herbivorous insects by Heteroptera predators (Agrawal et al. 1999; Eubanks and Denno 2000; Janssen et al 2003). To date, however, no study has considered modifying the foraging environment to increase the biological control efficiency on prey (i.e., often the target pests) by manipulating the resource inputs to the plants, such as nitrogen and water. We hypothesized that the enhanced plant growing status may facilitate the predation on prey items by Heteroptera predators. Thus, to test this hypothesis, the study model was presented in Fig. 4. In the article 5, we examined the effects of nitrogen and water inputs to tomato plants (Solanum lycopersicum L.) on the predation strength of the omnivore Macrolophus pygmaeus Wagner (Hemiptera: Miridae) on egg of Ephestia kuehniella Zeller (Lepidoptera: Pyralidae) used as prey (Fig. 5). The study had theoretical implications since it allows testing our hypothesis and thus helps understand the predation strength of Heteroptera predators on plants suffering resources input. Practically, the findings from the current study may help optimize biological control efficiency of Heteroptera predators. 138 [SOURCE: Page_138]

Figure 4: The study model of article 5. The left diagram shows the general model, the right shows the biological model used in this study [SOURCE: Page_139]

Omnivorous predator                           Macrolophus pygmaeus [SOURCE: Page_139]

Prey herbivores                                  Ephestia kuehniella [SOURCE: Page_139]

Solanum lycopersicum Plant                                        (cv. Marmande) [SOURCE: Page_139]

Resources input                                    Nitrogen / Water [SOURCE: Page_139]

139 [SOURCE: Page_139]

Figure 5: Bioassays on Macrolophus pygmaeus inside the mesh bags [SOURCE: Page_140]

140 [SOURCE: Page_140]

Article 5 [SOURCE: Page_141]

Nitrogen and water limitation in tomato plants triggers [SOURCE: Page_141]

negative bottom-up effects on the mirid omnivorous [SOURCE: Page_141]

predator Macrolophus pygmaeus [SOURCE: Page_141]

Han Peng^1, Bearez Philippe^1, Adamowicz Stephane^2, Lavoir Anne-Violette^1,3, [SOURCE: Page_141]

Desneux Nicolas 1* [SOURCE: Page_141]

1 French National Institute for Agricultural Research (INRA), UMR1355, 400 Route [SOURCE: Page_141]

des Chappes, 06903, Sophia-Antipolis, France 2 French National Institute for Agricultural Research (INRA), UR1115 PSH (Plantes [SOURCE: Page_141]

et Systèmes de culture Horticole), 84000 Avignon, France 3 University of Nice Sophia-Antipolis (UNS), UMR1355, 400 Route des Chappes, [SOURCE: Page_141]

06903, Sophia-Antipolis, France [SOURCE: Page_141]

* Corresponding author: [EMAIL_REDACTED] [SOURCE: Page_141]

141 [SOURCE: Page_141]

Abstract: Omnivorous Heteroptera play important roles in biological control of a [SOURCE: Page_142]

wide range of pest insects. Their predation could be influenced by various biotic or [SOURCE: Page_142]

abiotic factors. Here we examined the effects of nitrogen and water inputs to tomato [SOURCE: Page_142]

plants (Solanum lycopersicum L.) on the predation strength of the omnivore [SOURCE: Page_142]

Macrolophus pygmaeus Wagner (Hemiptera: Miridae) on eggs of Ephestia kuehniella [SOURCE: Page_142]

Zeller (Lepidoptera: Pyralidae) used as prey. We tested the hypothesis that a change in [SOURCE: Page_142]

plant status, manipulated by sub-optimal nutrients and water inputs, may change [SOURCE: Page_142]

predation on E. kuehniella by the predator M. pygmaeus. Nitrogen and water [SOURCE: Page_142]

restrictions depressed the predation of E. kuehniella eggs by M. pygmaeus. Moreover, [SOURCE: Page_142]

we observed a 30% reduction in the predator longevity on plants under low water [SOURCE: Page_142]

inputs. Therefore, the negative bottom-ups effect of nitrogen and water limitation (in [SOURCE: Page_142]

tomato plants) toward M. pygmaeus may be detrimental for mirid predator-based IPM [SOURCE: Page_142]

programs in tomato crops. [SOURCE: Page_142]

Key words: omnivory, tri-trophic interactions, predation, longevity, plant quality, [SOURCE: Page_142]

biocontrol [SOURCE: Page_142]

142 [SOURCE: Page_142]

Introduction [SOURCE: Page_143]

Arthropod is one of the most diverse groups in nature and this diversity could be [SOURCE: Page_143]

characterized by the wide range of feeding strategies they adopt. Among them, [SOURCE: Page_143]

omnivorous feeding strategy, i.e. omnivory plays an important role in community [SOURCE: Page_143]

dynamics (McCann et al. 1998; Eubanks and Denno 2000; Sinia et al. 2004). [SOURCE: Page_143]

Omnivorous predators can feed and damage plants while acting as predators of [SOURCE: Page_143]

herbivorous insects (Janssen et al. 2003). Omnivorous arthropods are increasingly [SOURCE: Page_143]

exploited for biological control in the sustainable integrated pest management (IPM) [SOURCE: Page_143]

because of their high efficacy in the suppression of pest populations (Coll 1998; [SOURCE: Page_143]

Margaritopoulos et al. 2003; Castañé et al. 2011; Chailleux et al. 2013a; Mollá et al. [SOURCE: Page_143]

2014). However, their efficacy may vary with biotic and abiotic variables (Symondson [SOURCE: Page_143]

et al. 2002). [SOURCE: Page_143]

Several Heteroptera species are omnivorous predators which need to acquire [SOURCE: Page_143]

nutrients and energy exploiting host plants and insect preys as well (Janssen et al. [SOURCE: Page_143]

2003). Phytophagous behaviour is common even if the frequency has not been [SOURCE: Page_143]

precisely assessed (Naranjo and Gibson 1996; Coll 1998; Kaplan and Thaler 2011). [SOURCE: Page_143]

Incidentally, changes in the plant nutritional status may affect the performance of [SOURCE: Page_143]

omnivorous Heteroptera. Lower plant quality induced by the manipulation of plant [SOURCE: Page_143]

organs (Eubanks and Denno 2000) or phytoalexins induction by earlier insect attacks [SOURCE: Page_143]

(Agrawal et al. 1999; Agrawal and Klein 2000) increased the predation on herbivores [SOURCE: Page_143]

by omnivores. In contrast, the improvement of plant quality by supplementing [SOURCE: Page_143]

high-quality food type, such as pollen, decreased the predation rate (Janssen et al [SOURCE: Page_143]

2003). However, little is known regarding how omnivorous predator could be [SOURCE: Page_143]

influenced by the variation in plant quality mediated by the manipulation of [SOURCE: Page_143]

143 [SOURCE: Page_143]

source-based nutrients, such as water and nitrogen inputs. Understanding this could be [SOURCE: Page_144]

valuable for optimizing the IPM strategies by manipulating the agricultural practices. [SOURCE: Page_144]

In this study, we hypothesized that a change in plant status, through manipulating [SOURCE: Page_144]

nutrient (N) and water inputs, may change predation strength on a given prey by an [SOURCE: Page_144]

omnivorous Heteroptera predator. The study was carried out using the biological [SOURCE: Page_144]

system: tomato (Solanum lycopersicum L.) as plant, the mirid Macrolophus pygmaeus [SOURCE: Page_144]

Wagner (Hemiptera: Miridae) as omnivorous predator, and eggs of [SOURCE: Page_144]

Ephestia kuehniella Zeller (Lepidoptera: Pyralidae) as food prey for the mirid [SOURCE: Page_144]

Macrolophus pygmaeus is well known to feed on plant vegetative organs [SOURCE: Page_144]

(Castañé et al. 2011) and is used as an efficient biological control agent against a wide [SOURCE: Page_144]

range of pests, including whiteflies, thrips, leafminers, aphids, mites and eggs of [SOURCE: Page_144]

Lepidoptera pests (Barnadas et al. 1998; Riudavets and Castañé 1998; Hansen et al. [SOURCE: Page_144]

1999; Margaritopoulos et al. 2003; Zappalà et al. 2013). It is also a predator of the [SOURCE: Page_144]

Lepidopteran pest, Tuta absoluta Meyrick (Biondi et al. 2013, Bompard et al. 2013, [SOURCE: Page_144]

Chailleux et al. 2013a, 2013b; Jaworski et al. 2013), a major invasive pest threatening [SOURCE: Page_144]

the tomato production worldwide (Desneux et al. 2010, 2011; Zappalà et al. 2013). [SOURCE: Page_144]

Indeed, in a companion study, we showed that variation in plant quality manipulated [SOURCE: Page_144]

by various levels of nitrogen and water inputs to the tomato plants did affect T. [SOURCE: Page_144]

absoluta survival and development (Han et al. 2014). Thus, understanding how the [SOURCE: Page_144]

plant nutritional inputs may also affect the predation of M. pygmaeus on tomato [SOURCE: Page_144]

Lepidoptera pests may provide an overview on S. lycopersicum - T. absoluta - [SOURCE: Page_144]

M. pygmaeus tri-trophic interactions to optimize IPM programme of this major pest. [SOURCE: Page_144]

144 [SOURCE: Page_144]

Materials and methods [SOURCE: Page_145]

Biological material [SOURCE: Page_145]

Tomato plants (Solanum lycopersicum L. cv. Marmande), were grown from seeds in a [SOURCE: Page_145]

climatic chamber (L:D 12:12, 241°C, 655% RH) as reported by Han et al. (2014). [SOURCE: Page_145]

Germination was carried out in small plastic pots (77 6.5 cm, TEKU, Rixheim, [SOURCE: Page_145]

France) filled with compost (Tonusol, Draguignan, France). In order to control [SOURCE: Page_145]

nutrition, the plantlets were transferred after rinsing the roots to pots containing an [SOURCE: Page_145]

inert substrate (perlite from Perlite Italianasrl, Corsico, Italy) the first time (T$_1$) at [SOURCE: Page_145]

DAS 8 (DAS - days after sowing, Fig. S$_1$), and the second time (T$_2$) to larger pots [SOURCE: Page_145]

(diam. 10 cm, height 9cm) at DAS 24. [SOURCE: Page_145]

Seven-day old Macrolophus pygmaeus female adults (BIOTOP, Valbonne, France) [SOURCE: Page_145]

were reared on tobacco plants in a mesh cage in a climatic chamber (L:D 16:8, 25 ± [SOURCE: Page_145]

1°C, 70 ± 10% RH). UV-irradiated Ephestia kuehniella (Zeller) (Lepidoptera: [SOURCE: Page_145]

Pyralidae) eggs are commonly used as factitious prey for the mass-rearing of M. [SOURCE: Page_145]

pygmaeus, and a recent study by Mollà et al. (2014) demonstrated how the food [SOURCE: Page_145]

regime composed by this prey and tomato plants, is suitable for the successful [SOURCE: Page_145]

M. pygmaeus population development. Therefore, in the present study, UV-irradiated [SOURCE: Page_145]

E. kuehniella eggs were used as prey for M. pygmaeus during all the bioassays. The [SOURCE: Page_145]

eggs were provided by BIOTOP (Valbonne, France) and were stored at 4°C until the [SOURCE: Page_145]

predation trials. [SOURCE: Page_145]

Plant nutrition: nitrogen and water inputs [SOURCE: Page_145]

Nutritional treatment design was adapted from Han et al (2014). From DAS 8, a full [SOURCE: Page_145]

nutrient solution adjusted to pH 5.5 was supplied daily to plants in a fully crossed [SOURCE: Page_145]

145 [SOURCE: Page_145]

design combining two nitrogen levels (ON vs. IN, optimal vs. insufficient nitrogen [SOURCE: Page_146]

respectively) with two water levels (OW vs. DT, optimal water vs. drought resp.). [SOURCE: Page_146]

Fifteen plants were subject to each treatment, i.e. 60 plants in total. [SOURCE: Page_146]

Nitrogen nutrition was determined a priori according to the principles of [SOURCE: Page_146]

programmed nutrient additions (Asher and Blamey 1987; Ingestad and Ågren 1988, [SOURCE: Page_146]

1992): ON inputs (Fig. S2A, closed symbols) were calculated on a daily basis as the N [SOURCE: Page_146]

amount required to obtain the maximum tomato growth and the highest leaf [N], as [SOURCE: Page_146]

previously determined by Han et al. (2014) under the same experimental conditions. [SOURCE: Page_146]

IN inputs (Fig. 2S A, open symbols) were calculated as ON/5. Water supplies [SOURCE: Page_146]

followed a “step increase” pattern (Fig. 2SB). OW was determined by empirical [SOURCE: Page_146]

evidence that the perlite substrate was fully-saturated and without leakage of nutrient [SOURCE: Page_146]

solution. DT was calculated as OW/3. [SOURCE: Page_146]

Bioassays [SOURCE: Page_146]

Plant growth status was evaluated non-destructively by monitoring plant height at [SOURCE: Page_146]

three dates (DAS 32, 40 and 58) and the number of nodes (DAS 58) (Fig.S$_1$ showed [SOURCE: Page_146]

the schedule of plant sampling and predation trials). [SOURCE: Page_146]

The predation behavior of M. pygmaeus females was assessed by comparing the [SOURCE: Page_146]

average daily consumption of E. kuehniella eggs on the plants treated with various [SOURCE: Page_146]

nitrogen and water treatments. Three successive predation trials (P$_1$, P$_2$ and P$_3$) were [SOURCE: Page_146]

performed (see Fig. S$_1$). In each trial, two randomly selected leaves were taken from [SOURCE: Page_146]

all plants and provided with 80 fresh E. kuehniella eggs (ten eggs on the abaxial and [SOURCE: Page_146]

adaxial surfaces of the four top leaflets). Each leaf was covered with a mesh-bag with [SOURCE: Page_146]

one 7-day old M. pygmaeus mated female, which were starved for 24 hrs prior to the [SOURCE: Page_146]

trials. The numbers of preyed eggs were counted as depleted egg shells after 48 hrs. [SOURCE: Page_146]

146 [SOURCE: Page_146]

Then, the egg shells were removed and 80 new E. kuehniella eggs were deposited for [SOURCE: Page_147]

the next trial. At last, 10 eggs were offered daily until the death of each predator in [SOURCE: Page_147]

order to estimate their longevity. Predators were considered dead when they remained [SOURCE: Page_147]

completely immobile after being touched with a fine paint brush. [SOURCE: Page_147]

Data processing [SOURCE: Page_147]

Shapiro and Bartlett tests were used to test variance homogeneity and normality of [SOURCE: Page_147]

residuals, respectively. Factorial two-way ANOVAs (Nitrogen x Water) were [SOURCE: Page_147]

performed with R software (R Development Core Team 2010) on plant heights [SOURCE: Page_147]

(measured at DAS 32, 40 and 58), node numbers (measured at DAS 58), total number [SOURCE: Page_147]

of eggs preyed by M. pygmaeus during the three trails and M. pygmaeus longevity. [SOURCE: Page_147]

The predation data from the three trials were summed. [SOURCE: Page_147]

Results [SOURCE: Page_147]

Plant growth [SOURCE: Page_147]

As expected from previous experiments in similar conditions (Han et al. 2014), [SOURCE: Page_147]

Table 1A shows that nitrogen treatments and interaction between nitrogen and water [SOURCE: Page_147]

had no significant effects on both criteria. Instead, the effects of water treatments were [SOURCE: Page_147]

significant (P< 0.001). [SOURCE: Page_147]

Figure 1 shows indeed that at DAS 58, similar plant heights and numbers of [SOURCE: Page_147]

nodes were observed under ON and IN, while the DT treatment restricted strongly [SOURCE: Page_147]

147 [SOURCE: Page_147]

both the plant heights (Fig. 1A) and the number of nodes compared to the OW (Fig. [SOURCE: Page_148]

## 1B).

Predation activity and longevity of M. pygmaeus [SOURCE: Page_148]

Both the nitrogen and water treatments had significant effects on the predation (Table [SOURCE: Page_148]

1B). No interaction effect “Nitrogen  Water” was found on both parameters. Indeed, [SOURCE: Page_148]

Fig. 2 shows that applying insufficient nitrogen and/or low water inputs to tomato [SOURCE: Page_148]

plants restricted strongly the predation of M. pygmaeus on the E. kuehniella eggs. [SOURCE: Page_148]

However, the predator longevity was only sensitive to the plant water status (Table [SOURCE: Page_148]

1B), with a marked reduction only under the DT treatment (Fig. 3). [SOURCE: Page_148]

Discussion [SOURCE: Page_148]

This experiment was designed to test the hypothesis that the changes in plant status [SOURCE: Page_148]

may influence the predation activity of an omnivorous Heteroptera. Indeed, we found [SOURCE: Page_148]

that the plants under sub-optimal nitrogen and water inputs did allow omnivorous [SOURCE: Page_148]

predators to consume significantly less preys (Fig. 2). Moreover, drought treatment [SOURCE: Page_148]

induced a reduction in longevity of the predator by nearly 30% compared to the [SOURCE: Page_148]

optimal water input (Fig.3). Overall, sub-optimal nitrogen and water input had a [SOURCE: Page_148]

negative effect on the omnivorous predators. [SOURCE: Page_148]

The effect of drought on plant growing status, i.e. smaller height and fewer [SOURCE: Page_148]

numbers of nodes, was clearly observed in present study (Fig. 1) and in our earlier [SOURCE: Page_148]

study as well (Han et al. 2014). In addition, M. pygmaeus may suffer a water [SOURCE: Page_148]

limitation on their predation behavior because they faced difficulties to acquire water. [SOURCE: Page_148]

148 [SOURCE: Page_148]

Among various benefits, omnivorous Heteroptera feed on plants to acquire the water [SOURCE: Page_149]

that required for the saliva production needed for the extra-oral digestion (Cohen 1998; [SOURCE: Page_149]

Sinia et al 2004; Gillespie and Mcgregor 2000). Thus, we could assume that in our [SOURCE: Page_149]

experiment, a water deficit in plant tissues restricted the predator ability to digest eggs, [SOURCE: Page_149]

which resulted in a longevity decrease (Fig. 3). [SOURCE: Page_149]

The nitrogen content of host plants often limits the performance of herbivorous [SOURCE: Page_149]

insects according to the “Nitrogen limitation hypothesis” (White 1993; Joern and [SOURCE: Page_149]

Behmer 1998). We confirmed this hypothesis with Tuta absoluta on tomato treated [SOURCE: Page_149]

with insufficient nitrogen input showing a decrease in leaf nitrogen content and an [SOURCE: Page_149]

increase in C/N ratio (Han et al. 2014). Here we observed that it concerns the [SOURCE: Page_149]

omnivorous predator M. pygmaeus as well, since significantly fewer eggs were preyed [SOURCE: Page_149]

on the plants treated with insufficient nitrogen input (Fig. 2). Unfortunately, little is [SOURCE: Page_149]

known about the feeding habits of omnivorous Heteroptera (Kaplan and Thaler 2011): [SOURCE: Page_149]

types of food (plant vs. prey) they prefer, plant parts they use, types of source they [SOURCE: Page_149]

exploit (cell content, phloem or xylem saps). Neither the injuries (feeding marks on [SOURCE: Page_149]

leaves or stems) done by M. pygmaeus have been identified yet (Castañé et al., 2011). [SOURCE: Page_149]

Thus, experiments allowing the direct observation of M pygmaeus feeding behaviour [SOURCE: Page_149]

(probing frequency, duration of feeding bouts, probing site selection) are clearly [SOURCE: Page_149]

required (Sinia et al. 2004). [SOURCE: Page_149]

At least two scenarios can be evoked to explain nitrogen effects: the first one is [SOURCE: Page_149]

trophic hypothesis where M. pygmaeus needs to feed on plants because they can [SOURCE: Page_149]

provide    some    nutrients    that   the    prey   animals     cannot    provide.    The [SOURCE: Page_149]

concentration/composition of these nutrients may vary depending on the nitrogen [SOURCE: Page_149]

input, even if they do not contain nitrogen itself, as nutrition alters the concentration [SOURCE: Page_149]

of a large range of organic and mineral compounds in tomato (Huanosto Magaña et al. [SOURCE: Page_149]

149 [SOURCE: Page_149]

2009). The second hypothesis is related to the plant chemical defense (see review by [SOURCE: Page_150]

Kaplan and Thaler 2011). Indeed, the concentration of many carbon based defence [SOURCE: Page_150]

compounds increases in the vegetative parts of tomatoes treated with low nitrogen [SOURCE: Page_150]

treatment (Le Bot et al. 2009; Larbat et al. 2012, 2014), thus restricting the [SOURCE: Page_150]

performances of herbivores and omnivores as well. [SOURCE: Page_150]

Taking as a whole the results of the present research and those of our companion [SOURCE: Page_150]

study (Han et al. 2014), we can conclude that optimal nitrogen and water inputs to the [SOURCE: Page_150]

tomato plants favored survival and development of the herbivore T. absoluta, and the [SOURCE: Page_150]

predation activity and longevity of an omnivorous predator M. pygmaeus. These [SOURCE: Page_150]

results certainly questioned the IPM strategies in terms of pest and natural enemy [SOURCE: Page_150]

population dynamics. More comprehensive works at a broader field scale on [SOURCE: Page_150]

arthropods population levels and on crop yields are required to definitely evaluate the [SOURCE: Page_150]

effects of nitrogen and water manipulations. [SOURCE: Page_150]

IV) Bottom-up effects and nitrogen and water on feeding ecology of an omnivorous predator [SOURCE: Page_164]

Our previous study (Article 5) suggested that both nitrogen deficiency and drought had a negative effect on predation strength of an omnivorous Heteroptera species M. pysmaeus. Moreover, drought significantly reduced their longevity. We concluded that plants under optimal growing status may enhance the performance of omnivorous predators. In practice, optimal fertilization practices should be adopted to ensure an optimal biological control effectiveness exerted by the predator. However, it is still unclear how these resource based manipulations may affect their feeding ecology, i.e., the functional relationship between plant feeding and prey feeding (Gillespie and Mcgregor 2000). Moreover, little is known about the feeding habits of omnivorous Heteroptera (Kaplan and Thaler 2011): types of food (plant vs. prey) they prefer and plant parts they use. For example, further experiments should be designed to allow direct observations on their plant and prey feeding behavior (i.e., frequency, duration of feeding bouts) (Sinia et al. 2014). The quantification of plant and prey feeding in omnivorous Heteroptera has not been performed in the context of manipulation of plant growing status through various levels of nitrogen and water inputs. Unlike other omnivorous arthropods, the predatory bugs from Heteroptera exhibit a relatively high incidence of omnivory. Nitrogen is an essential nutrient necessary to the synthesis of digestive enzymes, and water is required for the production of the saliva and for extracting the maximum nutrients from their prey while avoiding indigestible prey structures (Cohen et al. 1995; 1998). Therefore, we predicted that the sub-optimal inputs of nitrogen and water to plants may reduce the plant feeding by the omnivore, and in turn reduce its prey feeding (i.e., “Facilitation hypothesis”). Thus, to test this hypothesis, we the same study model iadopted in the previous study (Fig. 3). The study in article 6 is a companion study to the one in article 5. We examined the effects of variation in plant growing status, manipulated by contrasting levels of nitrogen (optimal N vs. insufficient N) and water (optimal water vs. drought) inputs, combined with the presence/absence of prey items, on (i) the feeding behaviour of a Heteroptera omnivore (plant feeding vs. prey feeding) (Fig. 6), (ii) plant preference behavior, and (iii) its life-history parameters: fertility and longevity. [SOURCE: Page_164]
