Article 6 [SOURCE: Page_166]

Obligatory phytophagy in an omnivorous predatory bug: a [SOURCE: Page_166]

case study supporting the facilitation hypothesis [SOURCE: Page_166]

Han Peng^1†, Dong Yong-cheng^1,2†, Lavoir Anne-Violette^1,3, Adamowicz Stéphane^4, [SOURCE: Page_166]

Bearez Philippe^1, Wajnberg Eric^1, Desneux Nicolas^1* [SOURCE: Page_166]

1 French National Institute for Agricultural Research (INRA), UMR1355, 400 Route [SOURCE: Page_166]

des Chappes, 06903, Sophia-Antipolis, France 2 Hubei Insect Resources Utilization and Sustainable Pest Management Key [SOURCE: Page_166]

Laboratory, College of Plant Science and Technology, Huazhong Agricultural [SOURCE: Page_166]

University, Wuhan, 430070, China 3 University of Nice Sophia-Antipolis (UNS), UMR1355, 400 Route des Chappes, [SOURCE: Page_166]

06903, Sophia-Antipolis, France 4 French National Institute for Agricultural Research (INRA), UR1115 PSH (Plantes [SOURCE: Page_166]

et Systèmes de culture Horticole), 84000 Avignon, France [SOURCE: Page_166]

* Corresponding author: [EMAIL_REDACTED] † These two authors contributed equally to the study. [SOURCE: Page_166]

166 [SOURCE: Page_166]

Abstract: Omnivorous arthropods make dietary choices, i.e., feeding on plants vs. on [SOURCE: Page_167]

prey, according to the characteristics of the environment in which they forage (e.g., [SOURCE: Page_167]

availability of prey, plant and prey qualities, etc.). Such decision, and subsequent [SOURCE: Page_167]

impact on key life history traits of the predators, may be affected by the availability of [SOURCE: Page_167]

nutrients and water to plants. It may occur through bottom-up forces impacting their [SOURCE: Page_167]

prey, direct and indirect effects occurring owing to food uptake on the plants, and/or a [SOURCE: Page_167]

combination of both. In the present study, we examined the impacts of prey presence [SOURCE: Page_167]

(Lepidoptera eggs) and availability of nitrogen (N) and water to host tomato plants on [SOURCE: Page_167]

the omnivorous predator Macrolophus pygmaeus Wagner (Heteroptera: Miridae). We [SOURCE: Page_167]

studied the feeding behavior of the predator (plant vs. prey feeding) and the life [SOURCE: Page_167]

history traits (fertility and longevity) on individual plants subjected to contrasted [SOURCE: Page_167]

nitrogen fertilizing and watering regimes. In the absence of prey, the predator fed [SOURCE: Page_167]

equally on the plants treated with contrasting levels of nitrogen and water. By contrast, [SOURCE: Page_167]

the feeding rate on the plant decreased in presence of prey when plants suffered low [SOURCE: Page_167]

water input. In addition, the feeding rate on prey was positively correlated with that [SOURCE: Page_167]

on plant and the predator showed a strong preference toward plants bearing prey and [SOURCE: Page_167]

subjected to high water regime. The presence of prey as food enhanced fertility and [SOURCE: Page_167]

longevity of M. pygmaeus, but the plants under low water input actually decreased [SOURCE: Page_167]

predator longevity. Our results support the Facilitation Hypothesis: the use of water [SOURCE: Page_167]

taken from plants facilitates the prey feeding activity (e.g., digestion) by omnivorous [SOURCE: Page_167]

predators from Heteroptera family. Phytophagous activity in such predators is not a [SOURCE: Page_167]

choice but a need for high fitness. [SOURCE: Page_167]

Key words: Omnivory, predator, longevity, fertility, nitrogen, water [SOURCE: Page_167]

167 [SOURCE: Page_167]

Introduction [SOURCE: Page_168]

Omnivorous feeding strategy, i.e., absence of carnivore or herbivore feeding specialization, [SOURCE: Page_168]

plays an important role in arthropod community dynamics (Polis 1991; Naranjo and Gibson [SOURCE: Page_168]

1996; McCann et al. 1998; Eubanks and Denno 1999; Coll and Guershon 2002; Eubanks [SOURCE: Page_168]

2005; Lundgren 2009; Chubaty et al. 2014). Feeding on more than one trophic level balances [SOURCE: Page_168]

the diets of omnivores when alternative foods provide complementary nutrients (Bjorndal [SOURCE: Page_168]

1991; Polis and Strong 1996; Agrawal et al. 1999). Omnivorous arthropods play a major role [SOURCE: Page_168]

in regulating herbivore arthropod populations in various natural and managed ecosystems [SOURCE: Page_168]

(McMurty and Croft 1997; Coll 1998; Eubanks and Denno 2000; Coll and Guershon 2002; [SOURCE: Page_168]

Symondson et al. 2002). However, their fitness (and their efficiency in some extent) is [SOURCE: Page_168]

strongly dependent on characteristics of the environment in which they forage (Agrawal et al. [SOURCE: Page_168]

1999; Eubanks and Denno 1999; Eubanks and Denno 2000; Sina et al. 2003). [SOURCE: Page_168]

By definition, omnivorous predators need to acquire nutrients from both their host plants [SOURCE: Page_168]

and their prey (Janssen et al. 2003); their dietary choice (e.g., feeding on plants vs. on prey) [SOURCE: Page_168]

may depend on habitat characteristics, notably the availability and density of prey (Agrawal [SOURCE: Page_168]

et al. 1999; Montserrat et al. 2004), the plant and prey qualities (Agrawal et al. 1999; [SOURCE: Page_168]

Eubanks and Denno 1999, 2000; Janssen et al. 2003), and abiotic factors such as water [SOURCE: Page_168]

availability (Gillespie and Mcgregor 2000; Sinia et al. 2003). For example, a poor plant [SOURCE: Page_168]

quality can promote consumption of prey eggs in western flower thrips (Agrawal et al. 1999; [SOURCE: Page_168]

Janssen et al. 2003) and in big-eyed bugs (Eubanks and Denno 1999; 2000). Such [SOURCE: Page_168]

dependence on plant quality implies that the feeding behaviour of these omnivores should be [SOURCE: Page_168]

affected by the actual availability of nutrients and water of the host plants through cascading [SOURCE: Page_168]

effects. Several studies assessed the effects of contrasting levels of water and nutrients [SOURCE: Page_168]

applied to host plants, on the performances of various herbivorous insects (Fischer and [SOURCE: Page_168]

168 [SOURCE: Page_168]

Fielder 2000; Inbar et al. 2001; Han et al. 2014), and of some omnivorous bugs (Groenteman [SOURCE: Page_169]

et al. 2006; Seagraves et al. 2011). However, the impact of plant quality variation mediated [SOURCE: Page_169]

by resource inputs (e.g., water and nutrients) on both the feeding behavior and life-history [SOURCE: Page_169]

traits of key omnivorous predators have been scarcely documented. [SOURCE: Page_169]

A theoretical framework with three contrasting hypotheses was proposed by Gillespie [SOURCE: Page_169]

and Mcgregor (2000) to describe the main relationships between plant and prey feeding [SOURCE: Page_169]

activities of omnivorous predators: (i) Switching hypothesis - the omnivorous predators [SOURCE: Page_169]

switch between plant and prey foods and thus, the prey feeding rate increases as plant feeding [SOURCE: Page_169]

rate decreases (e.g., when the plant quality is low); (ii) Facilitation hypothesis - plants [SOURCE: Page_169]

contain key components (nutrients or water) that facilitate prey feeding through digestion or [SOURCE: Page_169]

assimilation and thus, the prey feeding rate increases as plant feeding rate increases; (iii) [SOURCE: Page_169]

Independence hypothesis - the prey and plant feeding rates are independent. Most studies [SOURCE: Page_169]

support the Switching hypothesis when plant quality is low (i.e., lower palatability and higher [SOURCE: Page_169]

plant defense level) (Coll 1996; Agrawal et al. 1999; Eubanks and Denno 1999; Jassen et al. [SOURCE: Page_169]

2003; Eubanks 2005; Kaplan and Thaler 2011), but one study supports the Facilitation [SOURCE: Page_169]

hypothesis (Sinia et al. 2004) by showing that water acquisition through plant feeding by an [SOURCE: Page_169]

omnivorous predator is essential for its complete feeding on prey. [SOURCE: Page_169]

Phytophagous activity is common in the omnivorous predators belonging to the [SOURCE: Page_169]

Heteroptera sub-order. However, the prevalence of such activity in most groups has not been [SOURCE: Page_169]

precisely studied (Naranjo and Gibson 1996; Coll 1998; Kaplan and Thaler 2011). Unlike [SOURCE: Page_169]

other omnivorous arthropods, the predatory bugs from Heteroptera exhibit a relatively high [SOURCE: Page_169]

incidence of omnivory. It relates likely to their complex ingestion mechanism involving an [SOURCE: Page_169]

extra-oral enzymatic digestion process termed “solid-to-liquid feeding” (Cohen 2000). In this [SOURCE: Page_169]

respect, nitrogen is an essential nutrient necessary to the synthesis of digestive enzymes, and [SOURCE: Page_169]

169 [SOURCE: Page_169]

water is required for the production of the saliva and for extracting the maximum nutrients [SOURCE: Page_170]

from their prey while avoiding indigestible prey structures (Cohen et al. 1995; 1998). We [SOURCE: Page_170]

may hypothesize that Heteroptera predators face difficulties in ingesting and/or digesting prey [SOURCE: Page_170]

items on plants suffering drought and/or nitrogen deficiencies, thus resulting in lower prey [SOURCE: Page_170]

feeding activity. Assessing the frequency of plant vs. prey feeding with changes in plant [SOURCE: Page_170]

quality mediated by the manipulation of water and nitrogen inputs, may help understanding [SOURCE: Page_170]

the feeding ecology of such omnivores. Furthermore, it may be helpful for optimal use of [SOURCE: Page_170]

Heteroptera predators in biological control programs. [SOURCE: Page_170]

Therefore, we studied the combined impacts of contrasted host plant quality, by [SOURCE: Page_170]

manipulating levels of nitrogen (high vs. deficiency) and water (high vs. low), and prey [SOURCE: Page_170]

availability (presence vs. absence), on (i) the feeding activity of an Heteropteran omnivorous [SOURCE: Page_170]

predator, (ii) plant preference, and (iii) key predator life-history traits: fertility and longevity. [SOURCE: Page_170]

We hypothesized that plants subjected to sub-optimal nitrogen and water inputs may prompt a [SOURCE: Page_170]

decrease in plant utilization (i.e., feeding) by a Heteroptera omnivore, and in turn reduce its [SOURCE: Page_170]

prey feeding (i.e., facilitation hypothesis). The tri-trophic system “tomato (Solanum [SOURCE: Page_170]

lycopersicum) - eggs of Ephestia kuehniella Zeller (Lepidoptera: Pyralidae) - mirid predator [SOURCE: Page_170]

Macrolophus pygmaeus Wagner (Hemiptera: Miridae)” was employed to test this hypothesis. [SOURCE: Page_170]

Macrolophus pygmaeus is well-known to feed on plant vegetative organs (Castañé et al. 2011) [SOURCE: Page_170]

and is used as an efficient biological control agent against a wide range of pests, including [SOURCE: Page_170]

whiteflies, thrips, leafminers, aphids, mites and eggs of lepidopteran pests (Margaritopoulos [SOURCE: Page_170]

et al. 2003; Castañé et al. 2011; Chailleux et al. 2013). [SOURCE: Page_170]

170 [SOURCE: Page_170]

Material and Methods [SOURCE: Page_171]

Study organisms [SOURCE: Page_171]

Tomato plants (Solanum lycopersicum L. cv. Marmande), were grown from seeds in a [SOURCE: Page_171]

climatic chamber (L:D 12:12, 241°C, 655% RH) as reported by Han et al. (2014). [SOURCE: Page_171]

Germination was performed in small plastic pots (77 6.5 cm, TEKU, Rixheim, France) [SOURCE: Page_171]

filled with compost (Tonusol, Draguignan, France). In order to control nutrition, the plantlets [SOURCE: Page_171]

were transferred after rinsing the roots to pots containing an inert substrate (perlite from [SOURCE: Page_171]

Perlite Italiana srl, Corsico, Italy) the first time (T$_1$) at 8 DAS (DAS - days after seeding. see [SOURCE: Page_171]

Fig. 1) and the second time (T$_2$) to larger pots (diam. 10 cm, height 9cm) at 24 DAS. [SOURCE: Page_171]

On average seven-day old omnivorous predator Macrolophus pygmaeus female adults in [SOURCE: Page_171]

an average of seven-day old (BIOTOP Company, Valbonne, France) were reared on tomato [SOURCE: Page_171]

plants in a climatic chamber (L:D 16:8, 25 ± 1°C, 70 ± 10% RH). Ephestia kuehniella (Zeller) [SOURCE: Page_171]

eggs were commonly used as prey for the mass-rearing of M. pygmaeus. UV-irradiated E. [SOURCE: Page_171]

kuehniella eggs were used as prey for M. pygmaeus in the present study. The eggs were [SOURCE: Page_171]

provided by the BIOTOP Company (Valbonne, France) and were stored at 4°C prior to the [SOURCE: Page_171]

experiment. [SOURCE: Page_171]

Plant nutrition: nitrogen and water inputs [SOURCE: Page_171]

Nutritional treatment design was adapted from Han et al (2014). From 8 DAS, a full nutrient [SOURCE: Page_171]

solution adjusted to pH 5.5 was supplied daily to plants in a fully crossed design combining [SOURCE: Page_171]

two nitrogen levels (HN vs. LN, high vs. low nitrogen respectively) with two water levels [SOURCE: Page_171]

(HW vs. LW, high water vs. low water respectively). Each treatment was applied to 33 plants [SOURCE: Page_171]

(total 132). Among these, 24 plants (six per treatment) were free of insect as control. [SOURCE: Page_171]

171 [SOURCE: Page_171]

The nitrogen nutrition protocol was followed the principles of programmed nutrient [SOURCE: Page_172]

additions (Asher and Blamey 1987; Ingestad and Ågren 1988, 1992): HN inputs were [SOURCE: Page_172]

calculated on a daily basis as the nitrogen amount required to obtain the maximum tomato [SOURCE: Page_172]

growth and the highest leaf nitrogen concentration, as previously determined by Han et al. [SOURCE: Page_172]

(2014) under the same experimental conditions. LN inputs were calculated as HN/5. Water [SOURCE: Page_172]

supplies followed a “step increase” pattern. HW was determined by empirical evidence that [SOURCE: Page_172]

the perlite substrate was fully-saturated and without leakage of nutrient solution. LW was [SOURCE: Page_172]

calculated as OW/3 (see Fig S$_1$). [SOURCE: Page_172]

Plant feeding vs. prey feeding behavior [SOURCE: Page_172]

Figure 1 shows the schedule of all the experimental events. [SOURCE: Page_172]

The feeding observation of M. pygmaeus females was conducted by confining them [SOURCE: Page_172]

separately in a microcosm system i.e., small Petri dishes (diameter: 10 cm) with a lateral [SOURCE: Page_172]

ventilation hole (diameter: 7 cm) covered with fine nylon mesh screen. The females and the [SOURCE: Page_172]

terminal three leaflets of a tomato plant were enclosed in the Petri dish. Two treatments on [SOURCE: Page_172]

prey availability were performed: with and without E. kuehniella eggs as prey diet. The [SOURCE: Page_172]

behavior of M. pygmaeus was observed and recorded as: (i) plant feeding, (ii) prey feeding, [SOURCE: Page_172]

(iii) walking (or searching) and (iv) resting (or grooming). The piercing of leaf tissues with [SOURCE: Page_172]

the insect stylets was considered as “plant feeding”, while the piercing and sucking behavior [SOURCE: Page_172]

on eggs were recorded as “prey feeding”. The “walking” behavior mainly included searching [SOURCE: Page_172]

for food or shelter. The “resting" behavior mainly involves grooming of stylets or remaining [SOURCE: Page_172]

still. When a behavior was observed during 10 seconds, it was recorded and the next predator [SOURCE: Page_172]

was observed. The direct observation of each predator was performed twice daily (10h^00 [SOURCE: Page_172]

-11h^00 and 15h^00 -16h^00) during one week from 45 to 51 DAS (see Figure 1). After the data [SOURCE: Page_172]

172 [SOURCE: Page_172]

collection, the plant feeding rate was calculated as the proportion of records where M. [SOURCE: Page_173]

pygmaeus was observed as plant-feeding. The prey feeding rate was calculated following the [SOURCE: Page_173]

same logic. [SOURCE: Page_173]

Plant preference [SOURCE: Page_173]

The plant preference trial was conducted in a mesh cage (length  width  height: 211 m) [SOURCE: Page_173]

under greenhouse conditions (27 ± 3°C, 65 ± 10% RH). About 80 [SOURCE: Page_173]

M. pygmaeus adults (sexual ratio nearly 1:1, starved for 24 h) were released into a cage [SOURCE: Page_173]

containing plants treated with various nitrogen and water treatments. In each trial, eight [SOURCE: Page_173]

plants were used with one plant from each treatment (2  2  2: two nitrogen levels, two [SOURCE: Page_173]

water levels and two prey availabilities: presence or absence of E. kuehniella eggs). The [SOURCE: Page_173]

plants were arranged in a circle and the tested adults were released in the centre. The adults [SOURCE: Page_173]

were allowed 30 mins for choosing their plant habitat. Then, the numbers of adults found on [SOURCE: Page_173]

each plant were recorded. Ten trials (replicates) were performed. To avoid a bias resulting [SOURCE: Page_173]

from space preference of flying adults, the position of each plant treatment was randomly [SOURCE: Page_173]

affected in each trial. All trials were performed between 15h and 17h in a period of 4 days [SOURCE: Page_173]

## (52-55 DAS).

Predator fertility & longevity [SOURCE: Page_173]

Emerging nymphs within the microcosms were counted and removed daily in order to assess [SOURCE: Page_173]

the fertility of M. pygmaeus females. To estimate longevity, the predators were considered [SOURCE: Page_173]

dead when they remained completely immobile. [SOURCE: Page_173]

173 [SOURCE: Page_173]

Data processing [SOURCE: Page_174]

Factorial two-way ANOVAs (nitrogen  water) were performed on plant feeding rate in the [SOURCE: Page_174]

absence of prey, and on plant and prey feeding rate in the presence of prey in M. pygmaeus. [SOURCE: Page_174]

Factorial three-way ANOVAs (niteogen  water  prey) were performed on plant preference, [SOURCE: Page_174]

fecundity and longevity of M. pygmaeus. Linear regression analysis was conducted on plant [SOURCE: Page_174]

vs. prey feeding rates. Independent sample t-test assessed the effect of “water” and/or “prey” [SOURCE: Page_174]

on the plant preference, fertility and longevity when necessary. Shapiro and Bartlett tests [SOURCE: Page_174]

were used to assess variance homogeneity and normality of residuals, respectively. All the [SOURCE: Page_174]

datasets were analyzed using the R software (R Core Team, 2010). [SOURCE: Page_174]

Results [SOURCE: Page_174]

M. pygmaeus feeding behavior [SOURCE: Page_174]

Table 1A shows that in the presence of prey, the plant and prey feeding rate were affected [SOURCE: Page_174]

only by the water treatment. Furthermore, in this condition, Fig. 2A shows a significant [SOURCE: Page_174]

positive correlation between the plant and prey feeding rates (R$_2$ = 0.935, P = 0.034). On the [SOURCE: Page_174]

contrary, Table 1A and Fig. 2B show that in the absence of prey, the plant feeding rate was [SOURCE: Page_174]

neither affected by N or water inputs. [SOURCE: Page_174]

M. pygmaeus plant preference [SOURCE: Page_174]

Macrolophus pygmaeus plant preference was strongly affected by the water input and the [SOURCE: Page_174]

presence of prey, but not by the nitrogen input (Table 1B). Indeed, Fig. 3 shows that plants [SOURCE: Page_174]

suffering low water inputs attracted significantly less M. pygmaeus adults in both nitrogen [SOURCE: Page_174]

inputs (ON-OW vs. ON-DT: F = 0.686, t = 0.413, P = 0.002; IN-OW vs. IN-DT: F = 0.567, t [SOURCE: Page_174]

174 [SOURCE: Page_174]

= 5.504, P < 0.001). Furthermore, irrespective the nitrogen or water treatment, M. pygmaeus [SOURCE: Page_175]

adults moved significantly more towards plants bearing prey than plants without prey (Table [SOURCE: Page_175]

1B and Fig. 3). [SOURCE: Page_175]

M. pygmaeus fertility and longevity [SOURCE: Page_175]

The effect of the presence of prey was significantly positive on M. pygmaeus fertility (Table [SOURCE: Page_175]

1B). Indeed, Fig. 4A shows that the fertility was much lower in the absence of prey. On the [SOURCE: Page_175]

contrary, neither the nitrogen or water treatments had significant effects on the predator [SOURCE: Page_175]

longevity (Table 1B and Fig. 4A). [SOURCE: Page_175]

Table 1B shows that the effect of the presence of prey was also very highly significant on [SOURCE: Page_175]

longevity, and that, in addition, the water treatment was also significant on longevity. [SOURCE: Page_175]

Furthermore, Table 1B reveals a highly significant water  prey interaction. Indeed, Fig. 4B [SOURCE: Page_175]

shows that the presence of prey provokes a larger increase in the predator longevity on plants [SOURCE: Page_175]

that receive sufficient watering, than on plants suffering water limitation. [SOURCE: Page_175]

Discussion [SOURCE: Page_175]

This experiment was designed to test the “Facilitation hypothesis” which predicts that [SOURCE: Page_175]

sub-optimal nitrogen and water inputs to plants (i.e., limiting plant growth) reduce the plant [SOURCE: Page_175]

feeding rate by an omnivore, and consequently reduce also its prey feeding rate. Indeed, we [SOURCE: Page_175]

found that the prey feeding rate by M. pygmaeus was positively correlated with the plant [SOURCE: Page_175]

feeding rate (Fig. 1A). Water limitation applied to plants strongly limited both the plant and [SOURCE: Page_175]

the prey feeding rates by the omnivore. Additional findings showed that (i) in the presence of [SOURCE: Page_175]

175 [SOURCE: Page_175]

prey, water limitation induced a lower plant preference by M. pygmaeus and a lower [SOURCE: Page_176]

longevity, but did not affect fertility; (ii) the presence of prey strongly increased the fertility [SOURCE: Page_176]

and the longevity of M. pygmaeus. In addition, the predator preferred significantly more the [SOURCE: Page_176]

plants bearing prey to plants without prey. Thus, our results clearly support the predictions of [SOURCE: Page_176]

the “Facilitation hypothesis”. In the absence of prey, M. pygmaeus fed equally on the plants [SOURCE: Page_176]

treated with contrasting N and water inputs. In the presence of prey, we observed a positive [SOURCE: Page_176]

correlation between plant and prey feeding by M. pygmaeus. The water limitation treatment [SOURCE: Page_176]

decreased both the plant and prey feeding rates by the predator, which is consistent with our [SOURCE: Page_176]

previous study (Han et al., unpublished data) showing also a negative effect of drought on the [SOURCE: Page_176]

predation of prey eggs. Furthermore, our findings agree with the assumption by Sinia et al [SOURCE: Page_176]

(2004) that water acquired through plant feeding is an essential resource required for prey [SOURCE: Page_176]

predation. Among various benefits, omnivorous Heteroptera feed on plants to acquire the [SOURCE: Page_176]

water necessary to produce the saliva required for the extra-oral digestion of prey (Cohen [SOURCE: Page_176]

1998; Sinia et al 2004; Gillespie and Mcgregor 2000). [SOURCE: Page_176]

The low water treatment applied to plants affected the biological traits of M. pygmaeus. It [SOURCE: Page_176]

reduced significantly the predator longevity on plants bearing prey (Table 1B, Fig. 4B). Since [SOURCE: Page_176]

the predator needs water for prey digestion (Cohen 1995), drought may disturb this [SOURCE: Page_176]

physiological process and thus lead to decreased fitness and longevity. Also, the significant [SOURCE: Page_176]

prey  water interaction suggests that prey feeding increased the water stress in M. pygmaeus, [SOURCE: Page_176]

as also reported by Sinia et al (2004). Instead of being affected by nitrogen and water [SOURCE: Page_176]

treatments, M. pygmaeus fertility was dramatically and positively responsive to the presence [SOURCE: Page_176]

of prey (Fig 4A). Prey feeding has been proved to enable M. pygmaeus to achieve full [SOURCE: Page_176]

reproduction (Vandekerkhove and De Clercq 2010). Other predaceous bugs also need prey as [SOURCE: Page_176]

food sources to achieve optimal reproduction rates (Richards and Schidt 1996; Cocuzza et al. [SOURCE: Page_176]

176 [SOURCE: Page_176]

1997). Macrolophus pygmaeus adults chose much less plants under low water regime and [SOURCE: Page_177]

plants without prey (Table 1B, Fig. 3). This is probably because plants under low water [SOURCE: Page_177]

regime were smaller (not shown), and thus, had less chances to attract the omnivores. [SOURCE: Page_177]

Likewise, predators that were starved for 24 h were more likely to select the plants [SOURCE: Page_177]

supplemented with prey. [SOURCE: Page_177]

Besides ecological implications related to the stability of predator-prey population [SOURCE: Page_177]

dynamics (Eubanks and Denno 1999, 2000; Lalonde et al. 1999; Gillespie and Mcgregor [SOURCE: Page_177]

2000; Sinia et al. 2004), the facilitation hypothesis has applied implications because [SOURCE: Page_177]

omnivorous Heteroptera are biological control agents used in agriculture. Taking M. [SOURCE: Page_177]

pygmaeus as an example, such agents need to acquire water by feeding on plant tissue or sap [SOURCE: Page_177]

to facilitate prey predation and digestion, and, potentially, this may cause injury to plants [SOURCE: Page_177]

(Castañé et al. 2011). Thus, injuries might increase with increased densities of predators and [SOURCE: Page_177]

prey. In this context, plants face a trade-off between the costs (i.e., increase in injuries caused [SOURCE: Page_177]

by predators) and the benefits (i.e., decrease in damages caused by prey). Thus, special [SOURCE: Page_177]

attention should be paid to such trade-off before the application of these predators in an [SOURCE: Page_177]

augmentative biological control program. [SOURCE: Page_177]

In conclusion, our case study with the omnivorous Heteroptera M. pygmaeus clearly [SOURCE: Page_177]

supports the facilitation hypothesis stating that omnivorous Heteroptera derive water from [SOURCE: Page_177]

plants to facilitate prey feeding, and that phytophagy in omnivorous Heteroptera seems not a [SOURCE: Page_177]

choice but a necessity. Our study provides insights for future studies on feeding ecology and [SOURCE: Page_177]

into the suitability of use of omnivorous predators in biological control of pests: (1) Studying [SOURCE: Page_177]

the feeding ecology of omnivorous predators by manipulating the plant nutritional status [SOURCE: Page_177]

(through contrasting levels of nutrients, CO$_2$, water) may be valuable for other promising [SOURCE: Page_177]

omnivorous predators; (2) Direct observation of the feeding behaviour of omnivorous [SOURCE: Page_177]

177 [SOURCE: Page_177]

predators in confined microcosms can be used as a useful tool for understanding their feeding [SOURCE: Page_178]

ecology. [SOURCE: Page_178]

191 [SOURCE: Page_191]

Conclusion of chapter 3 [SOURCE: Page_192]

In summary, the following major conclusions can be drawn: (i) Improper nitrogen and water inputs triggered strong negative bottom-up effects on both herbivorous insects and omnivorous predator. Tuta absoluta, B. tabaci and M. pygmaeus performance survival/development were negatively affected with improper resources input to plants. The data supported “Nitrogen limitation hypothesis” and “Plant vigor hypothesis”. Nitrogen availability restricted survival and development of T. absoluta. Plants treated with optimal nitrogen and water inputs showed optimal growing status and favor the performance of T. absoluta. (ii) Other factors such as insect feeding strategy and plant cultivars (with resistant traits against nitrogen deficiency and drought) played important role in determining the consequences of these bottom-up effects. Tuta absoluta (leafminer) was affected by both excessive/insufficient nitrogen input and drought, but B. tabaci (phloem-feeder) was only affected by nitrogen deficiency-challenged plants. (iii) Plant resistance traits (i.e., drought adaptive and nitrogen deficiency regulation) did not necessarily mitigate the bottom-up effects of these stresses on herbivorous insects described in (i) and (ii). (iv) The omnivorous predator M. pygmaeus showed a sub-optimal predatory performance when plants were under nitrogen deficiency and/or drought. (v)   The omnivorous predator M. pygmaeus showed a positive correlation between plant feeding and prey feeding. The data supported “Facilitation hypothesis” that the plant feeding positively correlates with the prey feeding by omnivorous predators. Water acquired through plant feeding could be a limiting factor for facilitating prey feeding (i.e., predation) for them. [SOURCE: Page_192]

192 [SOURCE: Page_192]

Chapter 4: Nitrogen and water inputs mediated plant-arthropods Interactions: studies at population scale [SOURCE: Page_193]

193 [SOURCE: Page_193]

In the previous chapter, the bottom-up effects of nitrogen and water on plant-insects interactions have been examined at individual level. We found strong bottom-up effects of nitrogen and water inputs on performances of the tomato leafminer T. absoluta and whitefly B. tabaci, and omnivorous predator M. pygmaeus (conclusion of chapter 3). However, bottom-up effects by resource inputs and top-down forces by the natural enemies on herbivorous insects observed at the individual level do not always translate into population effects (Zaugg et al 2013). Thus, to completely understand how bottom-up and top-down forces interact on the herbivorous insects, it is necessary to design experiments at larger scales which allowed monitoring the population dynamics of both herbivorous insect and natural enemies. In chapter 4, we presented several studies which examined the bottom-up effects of plant resource inputs (i.e., nitrogen and water) and top-down effects of natural enemies on a target herbivorous pest species, either on tomato (Solanum lycopersicum L)      or cotton (Gossypium arboretum L). The study on tomato was performed under greenhouse conditions while study on cotton (Bt cotton vs. conventional cotton) was conducted in the open field. In the case of T. absoluta, the test system “Nitrogen / Water – Solanum lycopersicum – T. absoluta – M. pygmaeus” are expected to set up under greenhouse conditions. The herbivorous insect and predators were introduced into the system with tomato plants under various nitrogen and water inputs. Then the population dynamics of these two species can be monitored for predicting the relative strength of bottom-up forces vs. top-down forces (Study 7). Our study was not confined to studying the tomato agro-system. We performed larger-scale experiments on cotton under field conditions. Cotton (genus Gossypium） is an ecologically important crop widely grown in China (Wu and Guo 2005). The widespread adoption of insect-resistant genetically modified (IRGM) Bt cotton has led to decreased use of chemical insecticides and enhanced biocontrol services provided by natural enemies in Northern China (Lu et al. 2012). The Yangtze River Valley Cotton-planting Zone (YRZ) is one of the largest cotton-growing regions (Wan et al. 2008). In this region, several insect-resistant GM cotton cultivars, notably the transgenic cultivar that combines the two genes Cry^1Ac (Bt endotoxin) and CpTI (Cowpea Trypsin Inhibitor), have been widely [SOURCE: Page_194]

194 [SOURCE: Page_194]

commercially used during the past decade (Han et al. 2010a, 2010b; Chen et al. 2011). Secondary pest insects are the species less well-known, but of great concern and may cause economic consequences on plant production when the external conditions change. The cotton aphid Aphis gossypii Glover (Hemiptera: Aphidiae), non-targeted by Bt endotoxin, is considered as a secondary pest insect in this planting region. Cotton aphid may reach a high population density that causes serious damage to cotton (Wu and Guo 2005) owing to specific weather conditions (e.g., drought) or resistance to pesticides (Yi et al. 2012). Therefore, under the framework of IPM on A. gossypii, two primary research need to be performed: First, it is crucial to identify the guild of natural enemies potentially attacking A. gossypii in the Bt cotton agro-ecosystems in YRZ (Article 8). Identifying the key natural enemies against the targeted pest(s) in any cropping system is the first step for effectively implementing IPM programs (Norris et al. 2003; Liu et al. 2004; Desneux et al. 2010; Ragsdale et al. 2011). For example, identification of top-down forces (i.e., key natural enemies) in a given agro-ecosystems may help further research on how these natural enemies may be promoted for enhanced conservation biological control (Landis et al. 2000; Lundgren et al. 2009b; Wratten et al. 2012). Second, it is also important to study the bottom-up effects of resource input as well as top-down effects of natural enemy guild on the target pest A. gossypii (Article 9) based on the information of natural enemy guild obtained from previous studies. Furthermore, another source of bottom-up forces on A. gossypii population may come from plant resistance (Bt insecticidal proteins) via an indirect effect, i.e., the altered community structure of beneficial natural enemies in Bt cotton compared to a susceptible (i.e., conventional) cultivar (Lu et al. 2012). Thus, understanding the combined effects of all these forces in regulating the herbivorous pest has the potential to provide insights into fundamental ecological questions, as well as help guide the IPM programs of the target pest (Costamagna and Landis 2006; Costamagna et al. 2007). [SOURCE: Page_195]

195 [SOURCE: Page_195]

I) Bottom-up and top-down effects regulating a leafminer population [SOURCE: Page_196]

Our previous chapter presented strong bottom-up effects of nitrogen and water inputs on a herbivorous insect as well as an omnivorous predator. Because of the trophic link between herbivorous insect and the predator, it is difficult to predict the overall effects of these resources manipulations on the herbivorous insect. One recommended option is setting up a test system including plants, herbivorous insect and omnivorous predator. This design allows predicting the consequences of their interactions among them by monitoring their population dynamics. Thus, in study 7, the test model “water/nitrogen – Solanum lycopersicum – T. absoluta – M. pygmaeus” was set up under greenhouse conditions (Fig. 6). Water (high vs. low) and nitrogen (high vs. low) inputs are expected to be controlled by the automatic irrigation system. Tuta absoluta and M. pygmaeus were introduced into the system with tomato plants under four different treatments: HW-HN, HW-LN, LW-HN, LW-LN. The two species were firstly introduced into the cages with arranged plants for population establishment. Then, the population dynamics of the two species were monitored by counting the numbers of insects at various stages (Fig. 7). [SOURCE: Page_196]

198 [SOURCE: Page_198]

Introduction [SOURCE: Page_199]

The tri-trophic “Plant-herbivorous insect-natural enemy” interactions provides an ideal basic [SOURCE: Page_199]

model to understand how the plant-inhabiting arthropod community are structured by various [SOURCE: Page_199]

mechanisms (i.e. direct and indirect interactions) (Polis et al. 2000; Schmitz and Suttle 2001; [SOURCE: Page_199]

Shurin et al. 2002; van Veen et al. 2005). However, such interactions may be influenced by [SOURCE: Page_199]

plant-mediated bottom-up effects via plant genotypes (Johnson 2008), host plant resistance [SOURCE: Page_199]

(Walker and Jones 2001), and notably resources availability i.e. fertilization to plants (Denno [SOURCE: Page_199]

et al. 2002; Chau et al. 2005; Stiling and Moon 2005; Costamagna and Landis 2006; Couture [SOURCE: Page_199]

et al. 2010; Chen et al. 2010; Chow et al. 2012). Among the resources required by plants, [SOURCE: Page_199]

nitrogen and water availability have received most interests as they can exert a variety of [SOURCE: Page_199]

bottom-up effects and potentially alter tri-triphic interactions via various mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_199]

(Huberty and Denno 2004; Schoonhoven et al. 2005; Gutbrodt et al. 2011). Variations in plant [SOURCE: Page_199]

nutritional quality and plant defensive profile due to nitrogen/water input manipulations have [SOURCE: Page_199]

induced strong bottom-up effects on predator-prey (or parasitoid-host) relationships (Mattson [SOURCE: Page_199]

and Haack 1987; Inbar et al. 2001). [SOURCE: Page_199]

Insects, like other animals, need obtain nitrogen-based untrients (e.g. amino acid) and [SOURCE: Page_199]

water by plant feeding to ensure survival and growth (Schoonhoven et al. 2005). First, the [SOURCE: Page_199]

plant-inhibiting insects may suffer from water shortage and nitrogen limitation when the [SOURCE: Page_199]

plants are under water deficit (i.e. drought) (Han et al. 2014). First, plant tissues may become [SOURCE: Page_199]

less nutritious for insects when plants suffering water and nitrogen deficiency. In this case, [SOURCE: Page_199]

herbivorous insects may face difficulty in obtaining adequate water as the plant leaves tend to [SOURCE: Page_199]

199 [SOURCE: Page_199]

wilt, but also it restrict the uptake of nutrients as water acts as an indispensable ingredient for [SOURCE: Page_200]

mediating other nutrient availability (e.g. amino acid, soluble carbohydrates, lipid etc.) [SOURCE: Page_200]

(Douglas 2003; Huberty and Denno 2004). Second, water limitation to plants may allow them [SOURCE: Page_200]

produce higher defensive compounds such as secondary defense metabolites (Gutbrodt et al [SOURCE: Page_200]

2011). Likewise, nitrogen deficiency in the rooting medium has been shown to induce higher [SOURCE: Page_200]

levels of phenolics in plants such as tomato (Inbar et al. 2001; Le Bot et al 2009; Larbat et al [SOURCE: Page_200]

2012; Royer et al 2013). Hence, water/nitrogen deficiency may act in combination and induce [SOURCE: Page_200]

negative bottom-up effects on pant-feeding insects. [SOURCE: Page_200]

The effects of water/nitrogen limitation on herbivorous insects (Price 1991; White 1993; [SOURCE: Page_200]

Fischer and Fielder 2000; Inbar et al. 2001; Huberty and Denno 2004; Mody et al, 2009; [SOURCE: Page_200]

Gutbrodt et al. 2011), as well as on natural enemies (Gillespie and McGregor 2000; Sinia et al. [SOURCE: Page_200]

2003; Groenteman et al. 2006; Seagraves et al. 2011) have been extensively studied. However, [SOURCE: Page_200]

little is known regarding the influences of water/nitrogen availability on plant- herbivorous [SOURCE: Page_200]

insect-natural enemy tri-trophic interactions. It becomes more necessary to investigate this [SOURCE: Page_200]

topic when the natural enemy is an omnivorous predator due to the fact that they may be [SOURCE: Page_200]

influenced by altered prey quality/quantity (i.e. trophic cascades), as well as by altered plant [SOURCE: Page_200]

quality (Agrawal et al. 1999; Eubanks and Denno 2000; Kaplan and Thaler 2011). [SOURCE: Page_200]

In our previous studies, the testing model “Tomao - Tuta absoluta” as well as “Tomato - [SOURCE: Page_200]

Lepidoptera eggs - Macrolophus pygmaeus” were used to study the bottom-up effects of [SOURCE: Page_200]

water availability on the performances of the herbivore (Han et al. 2014) as well as the [SOURCE: Page_200]

omnivorous predator (Artilcle 5 and 6). We found that nitrogen and water deficit negatively [SOURCE: Page_200]

affected the performance of both T. absoluta and M. pygmaeus at individual scale. Thus, in [SOURCE: Page_200]

200 [SOURCE: Page_200]

the current study, an integrated model “Tomato – T. absoluta – M. pygmaeus” was set up to [SOURCE: Page_201]

examine the population effects of these two variables by monitoring the population dynamics [SOURCE: Page_201]

of the two species in one system, and notably to identify the overall impacts on T. absoluta [SOURCE: Page_201]

populations. We hypothesize that (i) two-fold negative effects of low water input on the [SOURCE: Page_201]

omnivorous predator were expected: (1) the decreased quantity/quality of T. absoluta as prey, [SOURCE: Page_201]

and (2) the decreased suitability of plant food (i.e. lower plant quality), and that (ii) such [SOURCE: Page_201]

two-fold negative effect on the predator may benefit T. absoluta by offsetting partially the [SOURCE: Page_201]

negative bottom-up effects from water deficit. [SOURCE: Page_201]

Material and Methods: [SOURCE: Page_201]

Study organisms [SOURCE: Page_201]

Tomato plants (Solanum lycopersicum L. cv. Marmande) were grown from seeds in a climatic [SOURCE: Page_201]

chamber (L:D 12:12, 241°C, 655% RH). Germination was performed in small plastic pots [SOURCE: Page_201]

(77 6.5 cm, TEKU, Rixheim, France) filled with compost (Tonusol, Draguignan, France). [SOURCE: Page_201]

One week after sowing, the seedlings were transferred individually with each kept in a plastic [SOURCE: Page_201]

box containing an inert substrate (perlite from Perlite Italianasrl, Corsico, Italy). Routine [SOURCE: Page_201]

nutrient solutions were applied to each potted seedlings daily to ensure optimal growth until [SOURCE: Page_201]

5-week old (35 DAS, DAS: days after sowing). [SOURCE: Page_201]

A Tuta absoluta colony was reared on tomato plants in a climatic chamber (L:D 16:8, 25 [SOURCE: Page_201]

± 1°C, 70 ± 10% RH). Macrolophus pygmaeus female adults (BIOTOP, Valbonne, France) [SOURCE: Page_201]

201 [SOURCE: Page_201]

were reared on tomato plants under the same conditions. It has been proved to be efficient [SOURCE: Page_202]

predator on T. absoluta larvae and eggs (Chailleux et al. 2013; Mollá et al. 2014). [SOURCE: Page_202]

UV-irradiated Ephestia kuehniella (Zeller) (Lepidoptera: Pyralidae) eggs that commonly used [SOURCE: Page_202]

as prey for the mass-rearing of M. pygmaeus were provided by BIOTOP and kept in 4 °C to [SOURCE: Page_202]

maintain fresh prior to the experiment. [SOURCE: Page_202]

Nitrogen/Water treatments [SOURCE: Page_202]

The experiment was carried out in one of the compartments in the greenhouse located at [SOURCE: Page_202]

Sophia Antipolis INRA center in France. Ambient temperature, relative humidity inside the [SOURCE: Page_202]

greenhouse and solar radiation outside the greenhouse were monitored throughout the [SOURCE: Page_202]

experiment (see Figure 1). The fertilization/irrigation regimes were applied by an [SOURCE: Page_202]

automatically-controlled irrigation system. Starting from DAS 35, we manipulated the [SOURCE: Page_202]

nitrogen and water inputs to the tomato plants at two levels: high water (HW) vs. low water [SOURCE: Page_202]

(LW) inputs and high nutrient (HN) vs. low nutrient (LN) (Figure 2). Thus we have four [SOURCE: Page_202]

treatments: HW-HN, HW-LN, LW-HN and LW-LN. Compared to HW treatment, we [SOURCE: Page_202]

manipulate the LW by cutting off the irrigation manually every 24 hrs. Differences between [SOURCE: Page_202]

HN and LN were controlled by the system. For each irrigation treatment, six replicates [SOURCE: Page_202]

(cages/plots) were set up with four tomato plants in each cage. [SOURCE: Page_202]

Insect introduction [SOURCE: Page_202]

The experiments were initiated by the introduction of the predator M. pygmaeus and herbivore [SOURCE: Page_202]

T. absoluta into the cages with plants inside (Figure 2). For M. pygmaeus, the first [SOURCE: Page_202]

202 [SOURCE: Page_202]

introduction (randomly selected two males and four females from the M. pygmaeus colony) [SOURCE: Page_203]

and the second (two males and five females) were performed at 41 DAS and 49 DAS [SOURCE: Page_203]

respectively. For T. absoluta, the first introdution (randomly selected one male and two [SOURCE: Page_203]

females from the T. absoluta colony) and the second (two males and one female) were [SOURCE: Page_203]

performed at 49 and 56 DAS respectively. To ensure their population establishment, [SOURCE: Page_203]

UV-irradiated E. kuehniella eggs and well-grinding pollen were provided as food for M. [SOURCE: Page_203]

pygmaeus once per week during the two weeks (from 41 DAS to 56 DAS). On the two dates [SOURCE: Page_203]

of T. absoluta release, honey diluents (10% honey) were sprayed onto the plants as food [SOURCE: Page_203]

source for T. absoluta. On 61 DAS, i.e. one week after the second T. absoluta release, the [SOURCE: Page_203]

numbers of T. absoluta and M. pygmaues were checked and the populations were adjusted by [SOURCE: Page_203]

supplement the insects when necessary. In addition, heights of all the plants were measured at [SOURCE: Page_203]

II) Identification of top-down forces regulating cotton aphid population in field [SOURCE: Page_211]

Our previous studies have focused on examining the bottom-up effects of resource input on plant-arthropods interactions. The strong effects of water and nitrogen inputs to the plants have been observed on herbivorous insects, omnivorous predator and their population dynamics. However, in agro-ecosystems, top-down forces regulating herbivorous insects has been strongly debated since it was postulated as a dominant force shaping natural communities by Hairston et al. (1960). Several herbivorous pest species have been suggested to be primarily controlled via top-down influences of their natural enemy guild (Rosenheim et al. 2004a; Costamagna and Landis 2006; Costamagna et al. 2007). Top-down forces exerted by natural enemy guild can lead to trophic cascades that release plants from herbivory (Polis et al. 2000), which forms the theoretical foundation of biological control. Therefore, these key natural enemies (i.e., one or a group of arthropod species which primarily contribute to the reduction of herbivore population) were expected to be identified. Thus, in article 8, we monitored the cotton aphid A. gossypii population dynamics and identified the effect of natural enemies on cotton aphid population growth using various exclusion cages in transgenic Cry^1Ac+CpTI cotton field (Fig. 8). Exclusion cages with fine mesh netting (various sizes of openings) manipulate the entry of various types of natural enemies (i.e., predators, parastioids or both). The experiment was conducted in a cotton filed located in Ezhou, Hubei Province, China (Fig. 9). [SOURCE: Page_211]

Article 8 [SOURCE: Page_214]

Identification of top-down forces regulating cotton aphid population growth in transgenic Bt cotton in central China [SOURCE: Page_214]

Peng Han^1,2, Chang-ying Niu^1*, Nicolas Desneux^2* [SOURCE: Page_214]

214 [SOURCE: Page_214]

Identification of Top-Down Forces Regulating Cotton Aphid Population Growth in Transgenic Bt Cotton in Central China Peng Han^1,2, Chang-ying Niu^1*, Nicolas Desneux^2* 1 Hubei Key Laboratory of Insect Resources Application and Sustainable Pest Control, Plant Science & Technology College, Huazhong Agricultural University, Wuhan, China, 2 French National Institute for Agricultural Research (INRA), Sophia-Antipolis, France [SOURCE: Page_215]

Abstract The cotton aphid Aphis gossypii Glover is the main aphid pest in cotton fields in the Yangtze River Valley Cotton-planting Zone (YRZ) in central China. Various natural enemies may attack the cotton aphid in Bt cotton fields but no studies have identified potential specific top-down forces that could help manage this pest in the YRZ in China. In order to identify possibilities for managing the cotton aphid, we monitored cotton aphid population dynamics and identified the effect of natural enemies on cotton aphid population growth using various exclusion cages in transgenic Cry^1Ac (Bt)+CpTI (Cowpea trypsin inhibitor) cotton field in 2011. The aphid population growth in the open field (control) was significantly lower than those protected or restricted from exposure to natural enemies in the various exclusion cage types tested. The ladybird predator Propylaea japonica Thunberg represented 65% of Coccinellidae predators, and other predators consisted mainly of syrphids (2.1%) and spiders (1.5%). The aphid parasitoids Aphidiines represented 76.7% of the total count of the natural enemy guild (mainly Lysiphlebia japonica Ashmead and Binodoxys indicus Subba Rao & Sharma). Our results showed that P. japonica can effectively delay the establishment and subsequent population growth of aphids during the cotton growing season. Aphidiines could also reduce aphid density although their impact may be shadowed by the presence of coccinellids in the open field (likely both owing to resource competition and intraguild predation). The implications of these results are discussed in a framework of the compatibility of transgenic crops and top-down forces exerted by natural enemy guild. [SOURCE: Page_215]

Citation: Han P, Niu C-y, Desneux N (2014) Identification of Top-Down Forces Regulating Cotton Aphid Population Growth in Transgenic Bt Cotton in Central China. Top-Down Forces Regulating Cotton Aphid in Central China [SOURCE: Page_217]

Figure 1. Design of the field study. (A) The four different cage treatments, i.e. natural enemy exclusion degree, in the field study; Exclusion cages: prevented natural enemy (predators and aphid parasitoids) movement, Restriction cage: prevented predator movement but allowed aphid parasitoids to colonize the plants, Sham cage and No cage: allowed free access to the plants for all natural enemies. (B) Within- and among block design: the distance among treatments within a block was 3 m, and among blocks was 10 m. The experimental cotton field was 70 m^630 m. doi:10.1371/journal.pone.0102980.g^001 [SOURCE: Page_217]

III) Bottom-up and top-down effects regulating cotton aphid population in field [SOURCE: Page_224]

Our previous study (Article 8) demonstrated the importance of the top-down forces exerted by natural enemies, mainly the predator coccinellids and aphid parasitoids, on cotton aphid A. gossypii in Bt cotton field in China. However, the relative strength of top-down vs. bottom-up forces on A. gossypii still needs to be studied in order to develop IPM including such forces in a sustainable and comprehensive way, especially since various studies have already identified the importance of bottom-up forces (e.g., fertilization regime) on herbivore population dynamics (Stiling and Rossi 1997; Denno et al. 2002; Huberty and Denno 2004; Ai et al. 2011). Nitrogen fertilization and water have been showed to have strong bottom-up effects on arthropod communities (Chen et al. 2010; Gutbrodt et al. 2011). Moreover, plant genotype has been proved an important ecological factor shaping tri-trophic communities (Underwood and Rausher 2000; Johnson 2008; Grinnan et al. 2013). The plant resistance against a given group of herbivorous pests (e.g., transgenic Bt insecticidal protein) in plants is another potential factor influencing the complex interaction between plants and arthropod communities. Thus, in article 9, the study model was present as bellow (Fig. 10). The study in article 9 is a companion study with that reported in article 8. To manipulate top-down forces, various exclusion cages were employed to manipulate the entries of various types of natural enemies (i.e., allowing entries of predators, parasitoids or both). Two types of bottom-up forces were manipulated in the field experimental design: plant cultivar (Bt cotton and conventional cotton), nitrogen fertilization (no input, low input and high input). [SOURCE: Page_224]

224 [SOURCE: Page_224]
